# Logion 44

- **Witness status:** Coptic-only
- **Parallels:** PENDING ROBERT
- **State:** DRAFTED (G1 self-audit done — see notes)

## Greek

No Greek witness — Coptic alone.

## Coptic (Coptic Scriptorium `thomas.gospel` v6.2.0, CC BY 4.0; base sentences s0155–s0157, normalized text as in the base file)

ⲡⲉϫⲉⲓⲏⲥⲟⲩⲥ ϫⲉⲡⲉⲧⲛⲁϫⲓⲟⲩⲁ ⲁⲡⲉⲓⲱⲧ ⲥⲉⲛⲁⲕⲱ ⲉⲃⲟⲗ ⲛⲁϥ ⲁⲩⲱ ⲡⲉⲧⲛⲁϫⲓⲟⲩⲁ ⲉⲡϣⲏⲣⲉ ⲥⲉⲛⲁⲕⲱ ⲉⲃⲟⲗ ⲛⲁϥ ⲡⲉⲧⲛⲁϫⲓⲟⲩⲁ ⲇⲉ ⲉⲡⲡⲛⲉⲩⲙⲁ ⲉⲧⲟⲩⲁⲁⲃ ⲥⲉⲛⲁⲕⲱ ⲁⲛ ⲉⲃⲟⲗ ⲛⲁϥ ⲟⲩⲧⲉ ϩⲙⲡⲕⲁϩ ⲟⲩⲧⲉ ϩⲛⲧⲡⲉ

## English draft

Jesus said: Whoever blasphemes against the Father will be forgiven. And whoever blasphemes against the Son will be forgiven. But whoever blasphemes against the Holy Spirit will not be forgiven, either on earth or in heaven.

## Notes

- "Blasphemes against" renders ⲛⲁϫⲓⲟⲩⲁ (will blaspheme/curse, from ϫⲓⲟⲩⲁ "speak badly, blaspheme") in all three clauses; the same verb is kept the same way three times, and the draft does not vary it to "speak against" in one place and "curse" in another. The first clause reads ⲁⲡⲉⲓⲱⲧ with ⲁ- before "the Father" in the base's analysis; the second and third take ⲉ- (against) — the difference is noted, the rendering is not split over it.
- "Will be forgiven" renders ⲥⲉⲛⲁⲕⲱ ⲉⲃⲟⲗ ⲛⲁϥ (they will release/forgive for him), with ⲕⲱ ⲉⲃⲟⲗ "let go, forgive"; "will not be forgiven" renders the same phrase with ⲁⲛ — the negation is kept with the verb, not moved.
- "The Holy Spirit" renders ⲡⲡⲛⲉⲩⲙⲁ ⲉⲧⲟⲩⲁⲁⲃ (the Spirit who is holy), with the Greek loan ⲡⲛⲉⲩⲙⲁ; "holy" is kept as an adjective on "Spirit," not turned into a separate name.
- "Either on earth or in heaven" renders ⲟⲩⲧⲉ ϩⲙⲡⲕⲁϩ ⲟⲩⲧⲉ ϩⲛⲧⲡⲉ (neither in the earth/land nor in heaven), with ⲕⲁϩ "earth/land" and ⲡⲉ "heaven/sky"; both places are kept, earth first as the Coptic orders them.
- G1: checked sentence by sentence against base sentences s0155–s0157; Father/Son forgiven, Spirit not forgiven, earth/heaven order kept; no parallel citations added (PENDING ROBERT).

# Logion 45

- **Witness status:** Coptic-only
- **Parallels:** PENDING ROBERT
- **State:** DRAFTED (G1 self-audit done — see notes)

## Greek

No Greek witness — Coptic alone.

## Coptic (Coptic Scriptorium `thomas.gospel` v6.2.0, CC BY 4.0; base sentences s0158–s0163, normalized text as in the base file)

ⲡⲉϫⲉⲓⲏⲥⲟⲩⲥ ⲙⲉⲩϫⲉⲗⲉⲉⲗⲟⲟⲗⲉ ⲉⲃⲟⲗ ϩⲛϣⲟⲛⲧⲉ ⲟⲩⲧⲉ ⲙⲉⲩⲕⲉⲧϥⲕⲛⲧⲉ ⲉⲃⲟⲗ ϩⲛⲥⲉⲣϭⲁⲙⲟⲩⲗ ⲙⲉⲩϯⲕⲁⲣⲡⲟⲥ ⲅⲁⲣ ⲟⲩⲁⲅⲁⲑⲟⲥ ⲛⲣⲱⲙⲉ ϣⲁϥⲉⲓⲛⲉ ⲛⲟⲩⲁⲅⲁⲑⲟⲛ ⲉⲃⲟⲗ ϩⲙⲡⲉϥⲁϩⲟ ⲟⲩⲕⲁⲕⲟⲥ ⲛⲣⲱⲙⲉ ϣⲁϥⲉⲓⲛⲉ ⲛϩⲉⲛⲡⲟⲛⲏⲣⲟⲛ ⲉⲃⲟⲗ ϩⲙⲡⲉϥⲁϩⲟ ⲉⲧϩⲟⲟⲩ ⲉⲧϩⲛⲡⲉϥϩⲏⲧ ⲁⲩⲱ ⲛϥϫⲱ ⲛϩⲉⲛⲡⲟⲛⲏⲣⲟⲛ ⲉⲃⲟⲗ ⲅⲁⲣ ϩⲙⲡϩⲟⲩⲟ ⲙⲡϩⲏⲧ ϣⲁϥ·ⲉⲓⲛⲉ ⲉⲃⲟⲗ ⲛϩⲉⲛⲡⲟⲛⲏⲣⲟⲛ

## English draft

Jesus said: They do not gather grapes from thorns. And they do not gather figs from brambles, for they bear no fruit. A good man brings out a good thing from his store. A bad man brings out bad things from his bad store that is in his heart, and he speaks bad things. For out of the fullness of the heart he brings out bad things.

## Notes

- "They do not gather grapes from thorns" renders ⲙⲉⲩϫⲉⲗⲉⲉⲗⲟⲟⲗⲉ ⲉⲃⲟⲗ ϩⲛϣⲟⲛⲧⲉ, with ϫⲱⲱⲗⲉ "gather/pick," ⲉⲗⲟⲟⲗⲉ "grapes/vine-fruit," and ϣⲟⲛⲧⲉ "thorn bush"; "they do not gather figs from brambles" renders ⲟⲩⲧⲉ ⲙⲉⲩⲕⲉⲧϥⲕⲛⲧⲉ ⲉⲃⲟⲗ ϩⲛⲥⲉⲣϭⲁⲙⲟⲩⲗ, with ⲕⲱⲧϥ "pick/gather," ⲕⲛⲧⲉ "figs," and ⲥⲉⲣϭⲁⲙⲟⲩⲗ "bramble/thistle growth." Fork noted: both gathering verbs mean picking by hand; "harvest" would sound larger than either word says.
- "For they bear no fruit" renders ⲙⲉⲩϯⲕⲁⲣⲡⲟⲥ ⲅⲁⲣ (for they do not give fruit), with ϯ "give" and the Greek loan ⲕⲁⲣⲡⲟⲥ "fruit" — "bear" is kept plain for ϯ here; the literal "give fruit" is on the page in this note.
- "Store" renders ⲁϩⲟ (store/treasure, what is laid up) in both halves; "treasure" is the other honest road, named here not chosen — "store" is kept because the saying is about what is brought out, not about wealth. "A good thing" renders ⲟⲩⲁⲅⲁⲑⲟⲛ and "bad things" renders ϩⲉⲛⲡⲟⲛⲏⲣⲟⲛ, both Greek loans (agathon / ponēron) kept plain as "good" and "bad."
- "That is in his heart" renders ⲉⲧϩⲟⲟⲩ ⲉⲧϩⲛⲡⲉϥϩⲏⲧ (the bad one that is in his heart) — the bad store is located in the heart by the Coptic, and the draft keeps that location rather than moving it to a separate clause.
- "And he speaks bad things" renders ⲁⲩⲱ ⲛϥϫⲱ ⲛϩⲉⲛⲡⲟⲛⲏⲣⲟⲛ, with ϫⲱ "say/speak"; the speaking is kept — the bad store shows in both what is brought out and what is said.
- "Out of the fullness of the heart" renders ⲉⲃⲟⲗ ϩⲙⲡϩⲟⲩⲟ ⲙⲡϩⲏⲧ, with ϩⲟⲩⲟ "excess/fullness/overflow" and ϩⲏⲧ "heart"; "abundance" is the other road. The closing line repeats "brings out bad things" as the Coptic repeats ⲉⲓⲛⲉ ⲉⲃⲟⲗ ⲛϩⲉⲛⲡⲟⲛⲏⲣⲟⲛ — the repeat is kept, not varied away.
- Doublet discipline: this logion's store/heart material is worked on its own worksheet; no other saying's wording is imported into it.
- G1: checked sentence by sentence against base sentences s0158–s0163; grapes/figs, good-store/bad-store, and fullness-of-heart order kept; no parallel citations added (PENDING ROBERT).

Logion 46  (source worksheet: logion-046.md)

> DRAFT — working translation from the Coptic (and Greek where it survives), prepared with AI assistance; not reviewed by a Coptic scholar; not finished. Corrections invited and credited.

# Logion 46

- **Witness status:** Coptic-only
- **Parallels:** PENDING ROBERT
- **State:** DRAFTED (G1 self-audit done — see notes)

## Greek

No Greek witness — Coptic alone.

## Coptic (Coptic Scriptorium `thomas.gospel` v6.2.0, CC BY 4.0; base sentences s0164–s0166, normalized text as in the base file)

ⲡⲉϫⲉⲓⲏⲥⲟⲩⲥ ϫⲉϫⲓⲛⲁⲇⲁⲙ ϣⲁⲓⲱϩⲁⲛⲛⲏⲥ ⲡⲃⲁⲡⲧⲓⲥⲧⲏⲥ ϩⲛⲛϫⲡⲟ ⲛⲛϩⲓⲟⲙⲉ ⲙⲛⲡⲉⲧϫⲟⲥⲉ ⲉⲓⲱϩⲁⲛⲛⲏⲥ ⲡⲃⲁⲡⲧⲓⲥⲧⲏⲥ ϩⲓⲛⲁ ϫⲉⲛⲛⲉⲩⲟⲩⲱϭⲡ · ⲛϭⲓⲛⲉϥⲃⲁⲗ ⲁⲓϫⲟⲟⲥ ⲇⲉ ϫⲉⲡⲉⲧⲛⲁϣⲱⲡⲉ ϩⲛⲧⲏⲩⲧⲛ ⲉϥⲟ ⲛⲕⲟⲩⲓ ϥⲛⲁⲥⲟⲩⲱⲛⲧⲙⲛⲧⲣⲣⲟ ⲁⲩⲱ ϥⲛⲁϫⲓⲥⲉ ⲉⲓⲱϩⲁⲛⲛⲏⲥ

## English draft

Jesus said: From Adam to John the Baptist, among those born of women, there is no one higher than John the Baptist, so that his eyes would not be broken. But I said: whoever among you becomes a little one will know the kingdom, and he will become higher than John.

## Notes

- "From Adam to John the Baptist" renders ϫⲓⲛⲁⲇⲁⲙ ϣⲁⲓⲱϩⲁⲛⲛⲏⲥ ⲡⲃⲁⲡⲧⲓⲥⲧⲏⲥ, with ϫⲓⲛ "from" and ϣⲁ "to/until"; "the Baptist" is the Greek loan ⲃⲁⲡⲧⲓⲥⲧⲏⲥ, kept as a name-title, not turned into "the one who dips."
- "Among those born of women" renders ϩⲛⲛϫⲡⲟ ⲛⲛϩⲓⲟⲙⲉ (in the births of the women), with ϫⲡⲟ "birth / being born" and ϩⲓⲟⲙⲉ (here normalized ϩⲓⲟⲙⲉ, lemma ⲥϩⲓⲙⲉ "woman"); the draft says "born of women" plainly rather than copying the Coptic's plural "births."
- "There is no one higher than John" renders ⲙⲛⲡⲉⲧϫⲟⲥⲉ ⲉⲓⲱϩⲁⲛⲛⲏⲥ (there is not the one who is high above John), with ϫⲟⲥⲉ / ϫⲓⲥⲉ "be high, be lifted"; the same verb returns at the end ("will become higher than John," ϥⲛⲁϫⲓⲥⲉ ⲉⲓⲱϩⲁⲛⲛⲏⲥ) and is kept the same way both times — "greater" is the other honest road, named here not chosen, because the Coptic's word is height, not size.
- "So that his eyes would not be broken" renders ϩⲓⲛⲁ ϫⲉⲛⲛⲉⲩⲟⲩⲱϭⲡ ⲛϭⲓⲛⲉϥⲃⲁⲗ, with ⲟⲩⲱϭⲡ "break, shatter" and ⲃⲁⲗ "eye." This is the logion's hard clause: the literal road (eyes not broken/shattered) is kept in the draft and flagged here, rather than smoothing it into an idiom the worksheet cannot prove from the Coptic alone. Fork on the page, not hidden.
- "Whoever among you becomes a little one" renders ⲡⲉⲧⲛⲁϣⲱⲡⲉ ϩⲛⲧⲏⲩⲧⲛ ⲉϥⲟ ⲛⲕⲟⲩⲓ (the one among you who will become being small), with ⲕⲟⲩⲓ "small, little"; "little one" is kept as a person, not thinned to "becomes humble."
- "Will know the kingdom" renders ϥⲛⲁⲥⲟⲩⲱⲛⲧⲙⲛⲧⲣⲣⲟ, with ⲥⲟⲟⲩⲛ "know" and ⲙⲛⲧⲣⲣⲟ "kingdom" (king-ness); no object is added after "kingdom," as the Coptic adds none.
- "But I said" renders ⲁⲓϫⲟⲟⲥ ⲇⲉ (I said it, and/but), kept as Jesus quoting his own earlier word inside the saying; the draft does not merge the two halves into one flat claim.
- G1: checked sentence by sentence against base sentences s0164–s0166; Adam-to-John span, eyes clause, little-one / know-the-kingdom / higher-than-John order kept; no parallel citations added (PENDING ROBERT).

Logion 47  (source worksheet: logion-047.md)

> DRAFT — working translation from the Coptic (and Greek where it survives), prepared with AI assistance; not reviewed by a Coptic scholar; not finished. Corrections invited and credited.

# Logion 47

- **Witness status:** Coptic-only
- **Parallels:** PENDING ROBERT
- **State:** DRAFTED (G1 self-audit done — see notes)

## Greek

No Greek witness — Coptic alone.

## Coptic (Coptic Scriptorium `thomas.gospel` v6.2.0, CC BY 4.0; base sentences s0167–s0173, normalized text as in the base file)

ⲡⲉϫⲉⲓⲏⲥⲟⲩⲥ ϫⲉⲙⲛϭⲟⲙ ⲛⲧⲉⲟⲩⲣⲱⲙⲉ ⲧⲉⲗⲟ ⲉϩⲧⲟ ⲥⲛⲁⲩ ⲛϥϫⲱⲗⲕ ⲙⲡⲓⲧⲉ ⲥⲛⲧⲉ ⲁⲩⲱ ⲙⲛϭⲟⲙ ⲛⲧⲉⲟⲩϩⲙϩⲁⲗ ϣⲙϣⲉϫⲟⲉⲓⲥ ⲥⲛⲁⲩ ⲏ ϥⲛⲁⲣⲧⲓⲙⲁ ⲙⲡⲟⲩⲁ · ⲁⲩⲱ ⲡⲕⲉⲟⲩⲁ ϥⲛⲁⲣϩⲩⲃⲣⲓⲍⲉ ⲙⲙⲟϥ ⲙⲉⲣⲉⲣⲱⲙⲉ ⲥⲉⲏⲣⲡ ⲁⲥ ⲁⲩⲱ ⲛⲧⲉⲩⲛⲟⲩ ⲛϥⲉⲡⲓⲑⲩⲙⲉⲓ ⲉⲥⲉⲏⲣⲡ ⲃⲃⲣⲣⲉ ⲁⲩⲱ ⲙⲉⲩⲛⲟⲩϫⲏⲣⲡ ⲛⲃⲣⲣⲉ ⲉⲁⲥⲕⲟⲥ ⲛⲁⲥ ϫⲉⲕⲁⲁⲥ ⲛⲛⲉⲩⲡⲱϩ ⲁⲩⲱ ⲙⲉⲩⲛⲉϫⲏⲣⲡ ⲛⲁⲥ ⲉⲁⲥⲕⲟⲥ ⲛⲃⲣⲣⲉ ϩⲓⲛⲁ ϫⲉⲛⲉϥⲧⲁⲕⲉϥ ⲙⲉⲩϫⲗϭⲧⲟⲉⲓⲥ ⲛⲁⲥ ⲉϣⲧⲏⲛ ⲛϣⲁⲓ ⲉⲡⲉⲓⲟⲩⲛⲟⲩⲡⲱϩ ⲛⲁϣⲱⲡⲉ

## English draft

Jesus said: It is not possible for a man to mount two horses, or to stretch two bows. And it is not possible for a servant to serve two masters — or he will honor the one, and the other he will insult. No man drinks old wine and at once wants to drink new wine. And they do not put new wine into old skins, so that they will not burst. And they do not put old wine into a new skin, so that it will not spoil it. They do not sew an old patch on a new garment, for a tear will come to be.

## Notes

- "It is not possible" renders ⲙⲛϭⲟⲙ ⲛⲧⲉ- (there is no power/possibility for …) twice, with ϭⲟⲙ "power, ability"; the same frame opens both the horse/bow half and the servant half and is kept the same way, not varied to "no one can" in one place only.
- "Mount two horses" renders ⲧⲉⲗⲟ ⲉϩⲧⲟ ⲥⲛⲁⲩ, with ⲧⲉⲗⲟ "mount, ride up on" and ϩⲧⲟ "horse"; "stretch two bows" renders ϫⲱⲗⲕ ⲙⲡⲓⲧⲉ ⲥⲛⲧⲉ, with ϫⲱⲗⲕ "draw, stretch" and ⲡⲓⲧⲉ "bow." The feminine numeral form ⲥⲛⲧⲉ for "two" (with the bow) against ⲥⲛⲁⲩ (with the horses/masters) is grammar, not a different count, and the draft does not split it.
- "Serve two masters" renders ϣⲙϣⲉϫⲟⲉⲓⲥ ⲥⲛⲁⲩ, with ϣⲙϣⲉ "serve" and ϫⲟⲉⲓⲥ "master, lord"; "honor" renders the Greek loan verb ⲣⲧⲓⲙⲁ (do honor) and "insult" renders ⲣϩⲩⲃⲣⲓⲍⲉ (do hybris — insult, treat badly). The or-clause ⲏ (or) is kept: the Coptic gives the outcome as an either/or, not as two flat predictions.
- "No man drinks old wine and at once wants new wine" renders ⲙⲉⲣⲉⲣⲱⲙⲉ ⲥⲉⲏⲣⲡ ⲁⲥ ⲁⲩⲱ ⲛⲧⲉⲩⲛⲟⲩ ⲛϥⲉⲡⲓⲑⲩⲙⲉⲓ ⲉⲥⲉⲏⲣⲡ (ⲛ)ⲃⲣⲣⲉ, with ⲥⲱ "drink," ⲏⲣⲡ "wine," ⲁⲥ "old," ⲃⲣⲣⲉ "new," and the Greek loan ⲉⲡⲓⲑⲩⲙⲉⲓ "desire, want." "At once" renders ⲛⲧⲉⲩⲛⲟⲩ (in that hour/moment); fork noted — the Coptic's time word is kept rather than dropping it.
- The two skins clauses are kept in the Coptic's order and not collapsed: new wine is not put (ⲛⲟⲩϫ, "throw/cast/put") into old ⲁⲥⲕⲟⲥ (skin, Greek loan) lest they burst (ⲡⲱϩ "burst, tear"); old wine is not put into a new skin lest it spoil it (ⲧⲁⲕⲟ "spoil, ruin"), with "it" pointing to the skin as the Coptic's object ϥ allows — the spoil-direction is footnoted here because the pronoun can be read tightly either way, and the draft follows the skin reading without hiding the fork.
- "Sew an old patch" renders ϫⲗϭⲧⲟⲉⲓⲥ ⲛⲁⲥ, with ϫⲱⲗϭ "sew" and ⲧⲟⲉⲓⲥ "patch, piece"; "on a new garment" renders ⲉϣⲧⲏⲛ ⲛϣⲁⲓ, with ϣⲧⲏⲛ "garment, clothing" and ϣⲁⲓ as the base gives it (the "new" side of the pair — gloss held light here because the base's lemma is simply ϣⲁⲓ; the old/new pairing is carried by the patch/garment contrast as printed). "A tear will come to be" renders ⲟⲩⲡⲱϩ ⲛⲁϣⲱⲡⲉ (a bursting/tearing will be) — the same ⲡⲱϩ word as the skins' bursting, kept related in English.
- The whole logion is one saying with four two-things pictures; the worksheet keeps all four and does not trim any as a doublet of another — each picture stands on this page.
- G1: checked sentence by sentence against base sentences s0167–s0173; two-horses / two-bows, two-masters honor/insult, old-wine/new-wine, both skins clauses in Coptic order, and the patch clause all kept; no parallel citations added (PENDING ROBERT).

# Logion 48

- **Witness status:** Coptic-only
- **Parallels:** PENDING ROBERT
- **State:** DRAFTED (G1 self-audit done — see notes)

## Greek

No Greek witness — Coptic alone.

## Coptic (Coptic Scriptorium `thomas.gospel` v6.2.0, CC BY 4.0; base sentences s0174–s0175, normalized text as in the base file)

ⲡⲉϫⲉⲓⲏⲥⲟⲩⲥ ϫⲉⲉⲣϣⲁⲛⲥⲛⲁⲩ ⲣⲉⲓⲣⲏⲛⲏ ⲙⲛⲛⲟⲩⲉⲣⲏⲩ ϩⲙⲡⲉⲓⲏⲓ ⲟⲩⲱⲧ ⲥⲉⲛⲁϫⲟⲟⲥ ⲙⲡⲧⲟⲟⲩ ϫⲉⲡⲱⲱⲛⲉ ⲉⲃⲟⲗ ⲁⲩⲱ ϥⲛⲁⲡⲱⲱⲛⲉ

## English draft

Jesus said: If two make peace with each other in this one house, they will say to the mountain, "Move away," and it will move.

## Notes

- "If two make peace with each other" renders ⲉⲣϣⲁⲛⲥⲛⲁⲩ ⲣⲉⲓⲣⲏⲛⲏ ⲙⲛⲛⲟⲩⲉⲣⲏⲩ, with ⲥⲛⲁⲩ "two," the compound ⲣⲉⲓⲣⲏⲛⲏ "make peace" (the Greek loan ⲉⲓⲣⲏⲛⲏ, peace, with ⲣ- "make/do"), and ⲙⲛⲛⲟⲩⲉⲣⲏⲩ "with each other." The two stay two in the draft; they are not merged into "one" here.
- "In this one house" renders ϩⲙⲡⲉⲓⲏⲓ ⲟⲩⲱⲧ, with ⲏⲓ "house" and ⲟⲩⲱⲧ "one, single" — the house is one, the people in it are two, and the draft keeps both numbers where the Coptic puts them.
- "They will say to the mountain, 'Move away'" renders ⲥⲉⲛⲁϫⲟⲟⲥ ⲙⲡⲧⲟⲟⲩ ϫⲉⲡⲱⲱⲛⲉ ⲉⲃⲟⲗ, with ϫⲱ "say," ⲧⲟⲟⲩ "mountain," and ⲡⲱⲱⲛⲉ ⲉⲃⲟⲗ "move away, turn away"; "and it will move" renders ⲁⲩⲱ ϥⲛⲁⲡⲱⲱⲛⲉ with the same verb — the saying's promise is kept in the same word twice, not strengthened.
- Doublet discipline: this logion's two-making-peace material is worked on its own worksheet. The "make the two one" material elsewhere in Thomas (e.g., the one/two wording footnoted at Logia 11 and 22, and the later paired logion 106 noted in the source audit) is cross-referenced here only as a variant family — it is never merged into this saying, and this saying's house/mountain wording is not imported into any other worksheet.
- G1: checked against base sentences s0174–s0175; condition, house, mountain-command, and movement result kept in Coptic order; no parallel citations added (PENDING ROBERT).

# Logion 49

- **Witness status:** Coptic-only
- **Parallels:** PENDING ROBERT
- **State:** DRAFTED (G1 self-audit done — see notes)

## Greek

No Greek witness — Coptic alone.

## Coptic (Coptic Scriptorium `thomas.gospel` v6.2.0, CC BY 4.0; base sentence s0176, normalized text as in the base file)

ⲡⲉϫⲉⲓⲏⲥⲟⲩⲥ ϫⲉϩⲉⲛⲙⲁⲕⲁⲣⲓⲟⲥ ⲛⲉ ⲛⲙⲟⲛⲁⲭⲟⲥ ⲁⲩⲱ ⲉⲧⲥⲟⲧⲡ ϫⲉⲧⲉⲧⲛⲛⲁϩⲉ ⲉⲧⲙⲛⲧⲣⲣⲟ ϫⲉⲛⲧⲱⲧⲛ ϩⲛⲉⲃⲟⲗ ⲛϩⲏⲧⲥ ⲡⲁⲗⲓⲛ ⲉⲧⲉⲧⲛⲛⲁⲃⲱⲕ ⲙⲙⲁⲩ

## English draft

Jesus said: Blessed are the single ones, and the chosen, for you will find the kingdom — for you are from it, and again you will go there.

## Notes

- "Blessed are the single ones" renders ϩⲉⲛⲙⲁⲕⲁⲣⲓⲟⲥ ⲛⲉ ⲛⲙⲟⲛⲁⲭⲟⲥ, with the Greek loan ⲙⲁⲕⲁⲣⲓⲟⲥ "blessed" and ⲙⲟⲛⲁⲭⲟⲥ "single one, solitary one" (the term also carried at Logion 16, footnoted there as ⲙⲟⲛⲁⲭⲟⲥ "single ones" — the same rendering is kept here for consistency, and "solitary" is the other honest road, named not chosen). The plural is kept: these are single ones, plural people, not one abstract state.
- "And the chosen" renders ⲁⲩⲱ ⲉⲧⲥⲟⲧⲡ (and those who are chosen), with ⲥⲱⲧⲡ "choose / be chosen"; the draft keeps "the chosen" as people alongside the single ones, not merged into them.
- "You will find the kingdom" renders ⲧⲉⲧⲛⲛⲁϩⲉ ⲉⲧⲙⲛⲧⲣⲣⲟ, with ϩⲉ "find" and ⲙⲛⲧⲣⲣⲟ "kingdom." The address shifts to "you" (plural) mid-saying, as the Coptic does — the draft keeps the shift rather than smoothing the whole logion into third person.
- "For you are from it, and again you will go there" renders ϫⲉⲛⲧⲱⲧⲛ ϩⲛⲉⲃⲟⲗ ⲛϩⲏⲧⲥ ⲡⲁⲗⲓⲛ ⲉⲧⲉⲧⲛⲛⲁⲃⲱⲕ ⲙⲙⲁⲩ, with ⲉⲃⲟⲗ ⲛϩⲏⲧ- "from in," the Greek loan ⲡⲁⲗⲓⲛ "again," ⲃⲱⲕ "go," and ⲙⲙⲁⲩ "there." Origin and return are both kept; neither half is dropped.
- G1: checked against base sentence s0176; single ones / chosen, find-the-kingdom, from-it / go-there-again order kept; term consistency with Logion 16's ⲙⲟⲛⲁⲭⲟⲥ noted; no parallel citations added (PENDING ROBERT).

# Logion 50

- **Witness status:** Coptic-only
- **Parallels:** PENDING ROBERT
- **State:** DRAFTED (G1 self-audit done — see notes)

## Greek

No Greek witness — Coptic alone.

## Coptic (Coptic Scriptorium `thomas.gospel` v6.2.0, CC BY 4.0; base sentences s0177–s0180, normalized text as in the base file)

ⲡⲉϫⲉⲓⲏⲥⲟⲩⲥ ϫⲉⲉⲩϣⲁⲛϫⲟⲟⲥ ⲛⲏⲧⲛ ϫⲉⲛⲧⲁⲧⲉⲧⲛϣⲱⲡⲉ ⲉⲃⲟⲗ ⲧⲱⲛ ϫⲟⲟⲥ ⲛⲁⲩ ϫⲉⲛⲧⲁⲛⲉⲓ ⲉⲃⲟⲗ ϩⲙⲡⲟⲩⲟⲉⲓⲛ ⲡⲙⲁ ⲉⲛⲧⲁⲡⲟⲩⲟⲉⲓⲛ ϣⲱⲡⲉ ⲙⲙⲁⲩ ⲉⲃⲟⲗ ϩⲓⲧⲟⲟⲧϥ ⲟⲩⲁⲁⲧϥ ⲁϥⲱϩⲉ ⲉⲣⲁⲧϥ ⲁⲩⲱ ⲁϥⲟⲩⲱⲛϩ ⲉⲃⲟⲗ ϩⲛⲧⲉⲩϩⲓⲕⲱⲛ ⲉⲩϣⲁⲛϫⲟⲟⲥ ⲛⲏⲧⲛ ϫⲉⲛⲧⲱⲧⲛ ⲡⲉ ϫⲟⲟⲥ ϫⲉⲁⲛⲟⲛ ⲛⲉϥϣⲏⲣⲉ ⲁⲩⲱ ⲁⲛⲟⲛ ⲛⲥⲱⲧⲡ ⲙⲡⲉⲓⲱⲧ ⲉⲧⲟⲛϩ ⲉⲩϣⲁⲛϫⲛⲉⲧⲏⲩⲧⲛ ϫⲉⲟⲩ ⲡⲉ ⲡⲙⲁⲉⲓⲛ ⲙⲡⲉⲧⲛⲉⲓⲱⲧ ⲉⲧϩⲛⲧⲏⲩⲧⲛ ϫⲟⲟⲥ ⲉⲣⲟⲟⲩ ϫⲉⲟⲩⲕⲓⲙ ⲡⲉ ⲙⲛⲟⲩⲁⲛⲁⲡⲁⲩⲥⲓⲥ

## English draft

Jesus said: If they say to you, "Where did you come from?" say to them, "We came from the light — the place where the light came to be by itself. It stood, and it was revealed in their image." If they say to you, "Is it you?" say, "We are its children, and we are the chosen of the living Father." If they ask you, "What is the sign of your Father in you?" say to them, "It is movement and rest."

## Notes

- The logion is three if-they-ask exchanges, and the draft keeps all three in Coptic order (origin / identity / sign), with the question words kept as questions — the worksheet does not turn the catechism into flat statements.
- "We came from the light" renders ⲛⲧⲁⲛⲉⲓ ⲉⲃⲟⲗ ϩⲙⲡⲟⲩⲟⲉⲓⲛ, with ⲉⲓ "come" and ⲟⲩⲟⲉⲓⲛ "light"; "the place where the light came to be by itself" renders ⲡⲙⲁ ⲉⲛⲧⲁⲡⲟⲩⲟⲉⲓⲛ ϣⲱⲡⲉ ⲙⲙⲁⲩ ⲉⲃⲟⲗ ϩⲓⲧⲟⲟⲧϥ ⲟⲩⲁⲁⲧϥ, with ⲙⲁ "place," ϣⲱⲡⲉ "come to be," ϩⲓⲧⲟⲟⲧ- "by, through," and ⲟⲩⲁⲁⲧ- "itself, alone" — "by itself" keeps the light as its own source here, as the Coptic phrases it; no outside maker is added.
- "It stood, and it was revealed in their image" renders ⲁϥⲱϩⲉ ⲉⲣⲁⲧϥ ⲁⲩⲱ ⲁϥⲟⲩⲱⲛϩ ⲉⲃⲟⲗ ϩⲛⲧⲉⲩϩⲓⲕⲱⲛ, with ⲱϩⲉ ⲉⲣⲁⲧ- "stand," ⲟⲩⲱⲛϩ ⲉⲃⲟⲗ "be revealed, appear," and the Greek loan ϩⲓⲕⲱⲛ "image" (eikōn); "their image" keeps the plural possessive ⲧⲉⲩ- as printed — whose image is not named in the clause, and the draft does not name it.
- "Is it you?" renders ⲛⲧⲱⲧⲛ ⲡⲉ (is it you [plural]) — the question is compressed in the Coptic and the draft keeps it short rather than expanding it into "Are you the ones from there?" or any fuller road.
- "We are its children, and we are the chosen of the living Father" renders ⲁⲛⲟⲛ ⲛⲉϥϣⲏⲣⲉ ⲁⲩⲱ ⲁⲛⲟⲛ ⲛⲥⲱⲧⲡ ⲙⲡⲉⲓⲱⲧ ⲉⲧⲟⲛϩ, with ϣⲏⲣⲉ "children, sons," ⲥⲱⲧⲡ "chosen," ⲉⲓⲱⲧ "father," and ⲉⲧⲟⲛϩ "living." "Its" points back to the light as the antecedent the Coptic's ϥ- allows; the fork is noted here — the draft follows the light, not a supplied "his," because the nearest named source in the saying is the light.
- "What is the sign of your Father in you?" renders ⲟⲩ ⲡⲉ ⲡⲙⲁⲉⲓⲛ ⲙⲡⲉⲧⲛⲉⲓⲱⲧ ⲉⲧϩⲛⲧⲏⲩⲧⲛ, with ⲙⲁⲉⲓⲛ "sign"; "It is movement and rest" renders ⲟⲩⲕⲓⲙ ⲡⲉ ⲙⲛⲟⲩⲁⲛⲁⲡⲁⲩⲥⲓⲥ, with ⲕⲓⲙ "movement, motion" and the Greek loan ⲁⲛⲁⲡⲁⲩⲥⲓⲥ "rest." The pair is kept as a pair — movement first, rest second, as the Coptic orders them — and neither term is explained away into the other.
- Reader-lens note (not a rendering rule): Robert's resonance with the light/inside material (audit §4, Logia 3, 5, 24) is recorded in the audit as his lens only; this worksheet's renderings above follow the Coptic terms ⲟⲩⲟⲉⲓⲛ / ϩⲓⲕⲱⲛ / ⲕⲓⲙ / ⲁⲛⲁⲡⲁⲩⲥⲓⲥ as printed and are not bent toward that lens.
- G1: checked sentence by sentence against base sentences s0177–s0180; all three exchanges, light-place / stood / revealed sequence, children/chosen clause, and movement-and-rest sign kept; no parallel citations added (PENDING ROBERT).

# Logion 51

- **Witness status:** Coptic-only
- **Parallels:** PENDING ROBERT
- **State:** DRAFTED (G1 self-audit done — see notes)

## Greek

No Greek witness — Coptic alone.

## Coptic (Coptic Scriptorium `thomas.gospel` v6.2.0, CC BY 4.0; base sentences s0181–s0184, normalized text as in the base file)

ⲡⲉϫⲁⲩ ⲛⲁ·ϥ ⲛϭⲓⲛⲉϥⲙⲁⲑⲏⲧⲏⲥ ϫⲉⲁϣ ⲛϩⲟⲟⲩ ⲉⲣⲉⲧⲁⲛⲁⲡⲁⲩⲥⲓⲥ ⲛⲛⲉⲧⲙⲟⲟⲩⲧ ⲛⲁϣⲱⲡⲉ ⲁⲩⲱ ⲁϣ ⲛϩⲟⲟⲩ ⲉⲣⲉⲡⲕⲟⲥⲙⲟⲥ ⲃⲃⲣⲣⲉ ⲛⲏⲩ ⲡⲉϫⲁϥ ⲛⲁⲩ ϫⲉⲧⲏ ⲉⲧⲉⲧⲛϭⲱϣⲧ ⲉⲃⲟⲗ ϩⲏⲧⲥ ⲁⲥⲉⲓ ⲁⲗⲗⲁ ⲛⲧⲱⲧⲛ ⲧⲉⲧⲛⲥⲟⲟⲩⲛ ⲁⲛ ⲙⲙⲟⲥ

## English draft

His disciples said to him: On what day will the rest for the dead come to be? And on what day will the new world come? He said to them: What you are waiting for has come, but you do not know it.

## Notes

- "His disciples said to him" renders ⲡⲉϫⲁⲩ ⲛⲁϥ ⲛϭⲓⲛⲉϥⲙⲁⲑⲏⲧⲏⲥ, with ⲙⲁⲑⲏⲧⲏⲥ the Greek loan "disciple"; the two questions that follow are kept as two questions, in Coptic order — rest first, new world second — not collapsed into one.
- "On what day will the rest for the dead come to be?" renders ⲁϣ ⲛϩⲟⲟⲩ ⲉⲣⲉⲧⲁⲛⲁⲡⲁⲩⲥⲓⲥ ⲛⲛⲉⲧⲙⲟⲟⲩⲧ ⲛⲁϣⲱⲡⲉ, with ⲁϣ "what, which," ϩⲟⲟⲩ "day," the Greek loan ⲁⲛⲁⲡⲁⲩⲥⲓⲥ "rest" (same word as the rest in Logion 50's "movement and rest," kept the same way here), ⲛⲉⲧⲙⲟⲟⲩⲧ "the dead ones," and ϣⲱⲡⲉ "come to be, happen." No day is named in the answer, and the draft names none.
- "And on what day will the new world come?" renders ⲁⲩⲱ ⲁϣ ⲛϩⲟⲟⲩ ⲉⲣⲉⲡⲕⲟⲥⲙⲟⲥ ⲃⲃⲣⲣⲉ ⲛⲏⲩ, with the Greek loan ⲕⲟⲥⲙⲟⲥ "world," ⲃⲣⲣⲉ "new" (in the base file's segmented form ⲃ-ⲃⲣⲣⲉ), and ⲛⲏⲩ / ⲉⲓ "come." "New world" keeps ⲕⲟⲥⲙⲟⲥ as "world," as elsewhere in this draft, rather than switching to "age" here alone.
- "What you are waiting for has come" renders ⲧⲏ ⲉⲧⲉⲧⲛϭⲱϣⲧ ⲉⲃⲟⲗ ϩⲏⲧⲥ ⲁⲥⲉⲓ, with ϭⲱϣⲧ ⲉⲃⲟⲗ ϩⲏⲧ- "look out for, wait for, expect" and ⲉⲓ "come." The Coptic's object is feminine (ⲧⲏ ... ⲥ, "that one [fem.]"), pointing back to the rest/world the disciples asked about; the plain draft says "what you are waiting for" rather than picking one of the two asked-about things and erasing the other.
- "But you do not know it" renders ⲁⲗⲗⲁ ⲛⲧⲱⲧⲛ ⲧⲉⲧⲛⲥⲟⲟⲩⲛ ⲁⲛ ⲙⲙⲟⲥ, with ⲥⲟⲟⲩⲛ "know" — the same knowing-word as Logia 46 and 49–50 — kept as plain "know," not softened to "recognize" or "realize" on this page.
- G1: checked sentence by sentence against base sentences s0181–s0184; both day-questions, the has-come answer, and the do-not-know close kept in order; no parallel citations added (PENDING ROBERT).

> DRAFT — working translation from the Coptic (and Greek where it survives), prepared with AI assistance; not reviewed by a Coptic scholar; not finished. Corrections invited and credited.

# Logion 52

- **Witness status:** Coptic-only
- **Parallels:** PENDING ROBERT
- **State:** DRAFTED (G1 self-audit done — see notes)

## Greek

No Greek witness — Coptic alone.

## Coptic (Coptic Scriptorium `thomas.gospel` v6.2.0, CC BY 4.0; base sentences s0185–s0188, normalized text as in the base file)

ⲡⲉϫⲁⲩ ⲛⲁϥ ⲛϭⲓⲛⲉϥⲙⲁⲑⲏⲧⲏⲥ ϫⲉϫⲟⲩⲧⲁϥⲧⲉ ⲙⲡⲣⲟⲫⲏⲧⲏⲥ ⲁⲩϣⲁϫⲉ ϩⲙⲡⲓⲥⲣⲁⲏⲗ ⲁⲩⲱ ⲁⲩϣⲁϫⲉ ⲧⲏⲣⲟⲩ ϩⲣⲁⲓ ⲛϩⲏⲧⲕ ⲡⲉϫⲁϥ ⲛⲁⲩ ϫⲉⲁⲧⲉⲧⲛⲕⲱ ⲙⲡⲉⲧⲟⲛϩ ⲙⲡⲉⲧⲛⲙⲧⲟ ⲉⲃⲟⲗ ⲁⲩⲱ ⲁⲧⲉⲧⲛϣⲁϫⲉ ϩⲁⲛⲉⲧⲙⲟⲟⲩⲧ

## English draft

His disciples said to him: Twenty-four prophets spoke in Israel, and all of them spoke in you. He said to them: You have left the Living One who is in front of you, and you have spoken about the dead.

## Notes

- "Twenty-four prophets spoke in Israel" renders ϫⲟⲩⲧⲁϥⲧⲉ ⲙⲡⲣⲟⲫⲏⲧⲏⲥ ⲁⲩϣⲁϫⲉ ϩⲙⲡⲓⲥⲣⲁⲏⲗ, with ϫⲟⲩⲧⲁϥⲧⲉ "twenty-four," the Greek loan ⲡⲣⲟⲫⲏⲧⲏⲥ "prophet," ϣⲁϫⲉ "speak," and ⲓⲥⲣⲁⲏⲗ "Israel." The number is kept as printed — twenty-four, not rounded and not explained on this page.
- "And all of them spoke in you" renders ⲁⲩⲱ ⲁⲩϣⲁϫⲉ ⲧⲏⲣⲟⲩ ϩⲣⲁⲓ ⲛϩⲏⲧⲕ, with ⲧⲏⲣⲟⲩ "all of them" and ϩⲣⲁⲓ ⲛϩⲏⲧ- "in, within." The draft keeps "in you" as the Coptic phrases it; the fork (in you / through you / about you) is named here — "in" follows the Coptic preposition ϩⲛ, and no smoother road is hidden.
- "You have left the Living One who is in front of you" renders ⲁⲧⲉⲧⲛⲕⲱ ⲙⲡⲉⲧⲟⲛϩ ⲙⲡⲉⲧⲛⲙⲧⲟ ⲉⲃⲟⲗ, with ⲕⲱ "leave, set aside, let go," ⲡⲉⲧⲟⲛϩ "the Living One" (ⲱⲛϩ "live" — the same title as the prologue's living Jesus, kept the same way), and ⲙⲧⲟ ⲉⲃⲟⲗ "presence, face, front." "Left" is kept plain; "ignored" or "passed over" would add a judgment the verb does not carry alone.
- "And you have spoken about the dead" renders ⲁⲩⲱ ⲁⲧⲉⲧⲛϣⲁϫⲉ ϩⲁⲛⲉⲧⲙⲟⲟⲩⲧ, with ϩⲁ "about, for, concerning" and ⲛⲉⲧⲙⲟⲟⲩⲧ "the dead ones" — the same word as the dead in Logion 51, kept the same way across the two logia, though the two sayings are not merged.
- Reader-lens note (not a rendering rule): Robert's resonance with the Living One / inside material (audit §4) is his lens only; the renderings above follow ⲕⲱ / ⲡⲉⲧⲟⲛϩ / ⲙⲧⲟ ⲉⲃⲟⲗ as printed and are not bent toward that lens.
- G1: checked sentence by sentence against base sentences s0185–s0188; twenty-four count, in-Israel / in-you pair, Living One / dead contrast kept in order; no parallel citations added (PENDING ROBERT).

Logion 53  (source worksheet: logion-053.md)

> DRAFT — working translation from the Coptic (and Greek where it survives), prepared with AI assistance; not reviewed by a Coptic scholar; not finished. Corrections invited and credited.

# Logion 53

- **Witness status:** Coptic-only
- **Parallels:** PENDING ROBERT
- **State:** DRAFTED (G1 self-audit done — see notes)

## Greek

No Greek witness — Coptic alone.

## Coptic (Coptic Scriptorium `thomas.gospel` v6.2.0, CC BY 4.0; base sentences s0189–s0191, normalized text as in the base file)

ⲡⲉϫⲁⲩ ⲛⲁϥ ⲛϭⲓⲛⲉϥⲙⲁⲑⲏⲧⲏⲥ ϫⲉⲡⲥⲃ︦ⲃⲉⲥⲃⲃⲉ ⲣⲱⲫⲉⲗⲉⲓ ⲏ ⲙⲙⲟⲛ ⲡⲉϫⲁϥ ⲛⲁⲩ ϫⲉⲛⲉϥⲣⲱⲫⲉⲗⲉⲓ ⲛⲉⲡⲉⲩⲉⲓⲱⲧ ⲛⲁϫⲡⲟⲟⲩ ⲉⲃⲟⲗ ϩⲛⲧⲉⲩⲙⲁⲁⲩ ⲉⲩⲥⲃⲃⲏⲩ ⲁⲗⲗⲁ ⲡⲥⲃⲃⲉ ⲙⲙⲉ ϩⲙⲡⲛⲉⲩⲙⲁ ⲁϥϭⲛϩⲏⲩ ⲧⲏⲣϥ

## English draft

His disciples said to him: Is circumcision useful, or not? He said to them: If it were useful, their father would father them from their mother already circumcised. But the true circumcision in the spirit has found all the use there is.

## Notes

- "Is circumcision useful, or not?" renders ⲡⲥⲃⲃⲉ ⲣⲱⲫⲉⲗⲉⲓ ⲏ ⲙⲙⲟⲛ, with ⲥⲃⲃⲉ "circumcision" (the base file prints the first occurrence with a combining mark, ⲥⲃ︦ⲃⲉ, reproduced as printed), the Greek-derived ⲣⲱⲫⲉⲗⲉⲓ "be useful, profit, help" (ōphelei), and ⲏ ⲙⲙⲟⲛ "or not." The question is kept as a short yes-or-no question, as asked.
- "If it were useful, their father would father them from their mother already circumcised" renders ⲛⲉϥⲣⲱⲫⲉⲗⲉⲓ ⲛⲉⲡⲉⲩⲉⲓⲱⲧ ⲛⲁϫⲡⲟⲟⲩ ⲉⲃⲟⲗ ϩⲛⲧⲉⲩⲙⲁⲁⲩ ⲉⲩⲥⲃⲃⲏⲩ, with the same ⲣⲱⲫⲉⲗⲉⲓ "useful" repeated from the question (kept the same word both times), ⲉⲓⲱⲧ "father," ϫⲡⲟ "father, beget" — the birth-word of Logion 46's "born of women," kept in the fathering sense here — ⲙⲁⲁⲩ "mother," and ⲉⲩⲥⲃⲃⲏⲩ "being circumcised." The condition is counterfactual in shape ("if it were useful ... would father"), and the draft keeps that shape rather than flattening it into a claim.
- "But the true circumcision in the spirit has found all the use there is" renders ⲁⲗⲗⲁ ⲡⲥⲃⲃⲉ ⲙⲙⲉ ϩⲙⲡⲛⲉⲩⲙⲁ ⲁϥϭⲛϩⲏⲩ ⲧⲏⲣϥ, with ⲙⲙⲉ "true" (of ⲙⲉ "truth"), the Greek loan ⲡⲛⲉⲩⲙⲁ "spirit," and ϭⲛϩⲏⲩ "find use, find profit" (ϭⲓⲛⲉ "find" + ϩⲏⲩ "use, profit"). "Found all the use" keeps ϩⲏⲩ tied to the useful-word of the question and answer; the other honest road ("has become wholly useful") is named here, not chosen, because the Coptic's verb is finding, not becoming.
- The saying answers with the same word three times — useful, useful, use — and the draft keeps that repetition rather than varying it away.
- G1: checked sentence by sentence against base sentences s0189–s0191; question, father/mother condition, and true-circumcision-in-spirit close kept in order; no parallel citations added (PENDING ROBERT).

# Logion 54

- **Witness status:** Coptic-only
- **Parallels:** PENDING ROBERT
- **State:** DRAFTED (G1 self-audit done — see notes)

## Greek

No Greek witness — Coptic alone.

## Coptic (Coptic Scriptorium `thomas.gospel` v6.2.0, CC BY 4.0; base sentence s0192, normalized text as in the base file)

ⲡⲉϫⲉⲓⲏⲥⲟⲩⲥ ϫⲉϩⲉⲛⲙⲁⲕⲁⲣⲓⲟⲥ ⲛⲉ ⲛϩⲏⲕⲉ ϫⲉⲧⲱⲧⲛ ⲧⲉ ⲧⲙⲛⲧⲣⲣⲟ ⲛⲙⲡⲏⲩⲉ

## English draft

Jesus said: Blessed are the poor, for yours is the kingdom of heaven.

## Notes

- "Blessed are the poor" renders ϩⲉⲛⲙⲁⲕⲁⲣⲓⲟⲥ ⲛⲉ ⲛϩⲏⲕⲉ, with the Greek loan ⲙⲁⲕⲁⲣⲓⲟⲥ "blessed" (the same blessed-word as Logion 49's "blessed are the single ones" and Logion 50's neighbors, kept the same way) and ϩⲏⲕⲉ "poor ones." The Coptic gives no added cause before the for-clause, and the draft adds none.
- "For yours is the kingdom of heaven" renders ϫⲉⲧⲱⲧⲛ ⲧⲉ ⲧⲙⲛⲧⲣⲣⲟ ⲛⲙⲡⲏⲩⲉ, with ⲙⲛⲧⲣⲣⲟ "kingdom" (kept as "kingdom," as throughout this draft) and ⲙⲡⲏⲩⲉ "of the heavens" — the Coptic noun is plural ("heavens"), rendered with the plain singular "heaven" as the ordinary English floor for the phrase, the plural noted here rather than made strange on the page. Fork on the page, not hidden: "kingdom of the heavens" is the more literal road.
- This is a single-sentence logion (s0192 alone) and is kept as a single sentence; it is not expanded with material from any other blessed-saying in Thomas.
- G1: checked against base sentence s0192; blessed / poor / yours / kingdom / heaven order kept; no parallel citations added (PENDING ROBERT).

# Logion 55

- **Witness status:** Coptic-only
- **Parallels:** PENDING ROBERT
- **State:** DRAFTED (G1 self-audit done — see notes)

## Greek

No Greek witness — Coptic alone.

## Coptic (Coptic Scriptorium `thomas.gospel` v6.2.0, CC BY 4.0; base sentences s0193–s0194, normalized text as in the base file)

ⲡⲉϫⲉⲓⲏⲥⲟⲩⲥ ϫⲉⲡⲉⲧⲛⲁⲙⲉⲥⲧⲉⲡⲉϥⲉⲓⲱⲧ ⲁⲛ ⲙⲛⲧⲉϥⲙⲁⲁⲩ ϥⲛⲁϣⲣⲙⲁⲑⲏⲧⲏⲥ ⲁⲛ ⲛⲁⲓ ⲁⲩⲱ ⲛϥⲙⲉⲥⲧⲉⲛⲉϥⲥⲛⲏⲩ · ⲙⲛⲛⲉϥⲥⲱⲛⲉ ⲛϥϥⲓ ⲙⲡⲉϥⲥⲧⲁⲩⲣⲟⲥ ⲛⲧⲁϩⲉ ϥⲛⲁϣⲱⲡⲉ ⲁⲛ ⲉϥⲟ ⲛⲁⲝⲓⲟⲥ ⲛⲁⲓ

## English draft

Jesus said: Whoever does not hate his father and his mother will not be able to be my disciple. And whoever does not hate his brothers and his sisters and carry his cross as I do will not be worthy of me.

## Notes

- "Whoever does not hate his father and his mother" renders ⲡⲉⲧⲛⲁⲙⲉⲥⲧⲉⲡⲉϥⲉⲓⲱⲧ ⲁⲛ ⲙⲛⲧⲉϥⲙⲁⲁⲩ, with ⲙⲉⲥⲧⲉ (lemma ⲙⲟⲥⲧⲉ) "hate," ⲉⲓⲱⲧ "father," and ⲙⲁⲁⲩ "mother." "Hate" is kept as the plain floor for ⲙⲟⲥⲧⲉ — the other roads ("reject," "leave behind") would soften a word the Coptic states hard, and the fork is named here rather than smoothed on the page.
- "Will not be able to be my disciple" renders ϥⲛⲁϣⲣⲙⲁⲑⲏⲧⲏⲥ ⲁⲛ ⲛⲁⲓ, with ϣ- "be able" and ⲣⲙⲁⲑⲏⲧⲏⲥ "become a disciple" (do-disciple; ⲙⲁⲑⲏⲧⲏⲥ the Greek loan of Logia 51–53, kept the same way). The first half ends on being a disciple; the second half ends on being worthy — the draft keeps the two different endings in the Coptic's order and does not level them into one condition.
- "And whoever does not hate his brothers and his sisters" renders ⲁⲩⲱ ⲛϥⲙⲉⲥⲧⲉⲛⲉϥⲥⲛⲏⲩ ⲙⲛⲛⲉϥⲥⲱⲛⲉ, with the same ⲙⲉⲥⲧⲉ "hate" repeated (kept the same word a second time), ⲥⲛⲏⲩ "brothers" (plural of ⲥⲟⲛ), and ⲥⲱⲛⲉ "sisters." The base file's mid-sentence "·" after "brothers" is reproduced in the Coptic block as printed and is not treated as a sentence break in the English.
- "And carry his cross as I do" renders ⲛϥϥⲓ ⲙⲡⲉϥⲥⲧⲁⲩⲣⲟⲥ ⲛⲧⲁϩⲉ, with ϥⲓ "carry, lift, take up," the Greek loan ⲥⲧⲁⲩⲣⲟⲥ "cross," and ⲛⲧⲁϩⲉ "in my way, as I do." "Carry" is kept over "take up" alone because ϥⲓ carries both and the plainer road is carrying; the fork is noted here.
- "Will not be worthy of me" renders ϥⲛⲁϣⲱⲡⲉ ⲁⲛ ⲉϥⲟ ⲛⲁⲝⲓⲟⲥ ⲛⲁⲓ, with the Greek loan ⲁⲝⲓⲟⲥ "worthy." No worth is explained on the page — the Coptic names the condition and the draft names nothing more.
- G1: checked sentence by sentence against base sentences s0193–s0194; father/mother-disciple condition and brothers/sisters-cross-worthy condition kept separate and in order; no parallel citations added (PENDING ROBERT).

# Logion 56

- **Witness status:** Coptic-only
- **Parallels:** PENDING ROBERT
- **State:** DRAFTED (G1 self-audit done — see notes)

## Greek

No Greek witness — Coptic alone.

## Coptic (Coptic Scriptorium `thomas.gospel` v6.2.0, CC BY 4.0; base sentences s0195–s0196, normalized text as in the base file)

ⲡⲉϫⲉⲓⲏⲥⲟⲩⲥ ϫⲉⲡⲉⲧⲁϥⲥⲟⲩⲱⲛⲡⲕⲟⲥⲙⲟⲥ ⲁϥϩⲉ ⲉⲩⲡⲧⲱⲙⲁ ⲁⲩⲱ ⲡⲉⲛⲧⲁϥϩⲉ ⲉⲡⲧⲱⲙⲁ ⲡⲕⲟⲥⲙⲟⲥ ⲙⲡϣⲁ ⲙⲙⲟϥ ⲁⲛ

## English draft

Jesus said: Whoever has known the world has found a corpse. And whoever has found the corpse, the world is not worthy of him.

## Notes

- "Whoever has known the world" renders ⲡⲉⲧⲁϥⲥⲟⲩⲱⲛⲡⲕⲟⲥⲙⲟⲥ, with ⲥⲟⲩⲱⲛ (lemma ⲥⲟⲟⲩⲛ) "know" — the same knowing-word as Logia 46, 49–51 — kept as plain "known," and the Greek loan ⲕⲟⲥⲙⲟⲥ "world," kept as "world" as throughout this draft.
- "Has found a corpse" renders ⲁϥϩⲉ ⲉⲩⲡⲧⲱⲙⲁ, with ϩⲉ "find" and the Greek loan ⲡⲧⲱⲙⲁ "corpse, fallen body." "Corpse" is kept as the plain floor; the fork ("carcass," "dead body") is noted here, and the same word is kept where ⲡⲧⲱⲙⲁ returns in Logion 60.
- "And whoever has found the corpse, the world is not worthy of him" renders ⲁⲩⲱ ⲡⲉⲛⲧⲁϥϩⲉ ⲉⲡⲧⲱⲙⲁ ⲡⲕⲟⲥⲙⲟⲥ ⲙⲡϣⲁ ⲙⲙⲟϥ ⲁⲛ, with ⲙⲡϣⲁ "be worthy" negated by ⲁⲛ. The Coptic apposition — the corpse, that is, the world — is kept in the word order the sentence gives: the second half names ⲡⲕⲟⲥⲙⲟⲥ "the world" right after "the corpse," and the draft does not turn that into a separate explanation.
- Doublet discipline: this corpse-and-world saying has a counterpart later in Thomas (Logion 80). Each occurrence stands on its own worksheet; the two are footnoted here, never merged into one improved saying.
- G1: checked sentence by sentence against base sentences s0195–s0196; know / find / corpse / not-worthy order kept in both halves; no parallel citations added (PENDING ROBERT).

# Logion 57

- **Witness status:** Coptic-only
- **Parallels:** PENDING ROBERT
- **State:** DRAFTED (G1 self-audit done — see notes)

## Greek

No Greek witness — Coptic alone.

## Coptic (Coptic Scriptorium `thomas.gospel` v6.2.0, CC BY 4.0; base sentences s0197–s0201, normalized text as in the base file)

ⲡⲉϫⲉⲓⲏⲥⲟⲩⲥ ϫⲉⲧⲙⲛⲧⲣⲣⲟ ⲙⲡⲉⲓⲱⲧ ⲉⲥⲧⲛⲧⲱⲛ ⲉⲩⲣⲱⲙⲉ ⲉⲩⲛⲧⲁϥ ⲙⲙⲁⲩ ⲛⲟⲩϭⲣⲟϭ ⲉⲛⲁⲛⲟⲩϥ ⲁⲡⲉϥϫⲁϫⲉ ⲉⲓ ⲛⲧⲟⲩϣⲏ ⲁϥⲥⲓⲧⲉ ⲛⲟⲩⲍⲓⲍⲁⲛⲓⲟⲛ ⲉϫⲛⲡⲉϭⲣⲟϭ ⲉⲧⲛⲁⲛⲟⲩϥ ⲙⲡⲉⲡⲣⲱⲙⲉ ⲕⲁⲁⲩ ⲉϩⲱⲱⲗⲉ ⲙⲡⲍⲓⲍⲁⲛⲓⲟⲛ ⲡⲉϫⲁϥ ⲛⲁⲩ ϫⲉⲙⲏⲡⲱⲥ ⲛⲧⲉⲧⲛⲃⲱⲕ ϫⲉⲉⲧⲉⲧⲛⲛⲁϩⲱⲱⲗⲉ ⲙⲡⲍⲓⲍⲁⲛⲓⲟⲛ ⲛⲧⲉⲧⲛϩⲱⲱⲗⲉ ⲙⲡⲥⲟⲩⲟ ⲛⲙⲙⲁϥ ϩⲙⲡϩⲟⲟⲩ ⲅⲁⲣ ⲙⲡⲱϩⲥ ⲛⲍⲓⲍⲁⲛⲓⲟⲛ ⲛⲁⲟⲩⲱⲛϩ ⲉⲃⲟⲗ ⲥⲉϩⲟⲗⲟⲩ ⲛⲥⲉⲣⲟⲕϩⲟⲩ

## English draft

Jesus said: The kingdom of the Father is like a man who has good seed. His enemy came during the night and sowed weeds on top of the good seed. The man did not let them pull up the weeds. He said to them: In case you go meaning to pull up the weeds and you pull up the wheat along with them — for on the day of harvest the weeds will show plainly; they will be pulled up and burned.

## Notes

- "The kingdom of the Father is like a man who has good seed" renders ⲧⲙⲛⲧⲣⲣⲟ ⲙⲡⲉⲓⲱⲧ ⲉⲥⲧⲛⲧⲱⲛ ⲉⲩⲣⲱⲙⲉ ⲉⲩⲛⲧⲁϥ ⲙⲙⲁⲩ ⲛⲟⲩϭⲣⲟϭ ⲉⲛⲁⲛⲟⲩϥ, with ⲙⲛⲧⲣⲣⲟ "kingdom" (kept as "kingdom," as throughout), ⲉⲓⲱⲧ "Father," ⲧⲛⲧⲱⲛ "be like, resemble," ⲣⲱⲙⲉ "man," and ϭⲣⲟϭ "seed" qualified by ⲛⲁⲛⲟⲩϥ "good." The supplied letters in the base at this point (ⲧ⟨ⲛ̄⟩ⲧⲱ(ⲛ)) are the edition's, noted here; no extra Coptic is invented on this page.
- "His enemy came during the night and sowed weeds" renders ⲁⲡⲉϥϫⲁϫⲉ ⲉⲓ ⲛⲧⲟⲩϣⲏ ⲁϥⲥⲓⲧⲉ ⲛⲟⲩⲍⲓⲍⲁⲛⲓⲟⲛ, with ϫⲁϫⲉ "enemy," ⲟⲩϣⲏ "night," ⲥⲓⲧⲉ "sow, scatter seed," and the Greek loan ⲍⲓⲍⲁⲛⲓⲟⲛ rendered "weeds." Fork named here: the loan names a specific weed-plant (often "tares / darnel"); the plain draft says "weeds" and does not pick a species the Coptic page itself does not name in English.
- "On top of the good seed" renders ⲉϫⲛⲡⲉϭⲣⲟϭ ⲉⲧⲛⲁⲛⲟⲩϥ, with ⲉϫⲛ "on, upon" and the same ϭⲣⲟϭ / ⲛⲁⲛⲟⲩϥ pair repeated; the repetition is kept, not varied away.
- "The man did not let them pull up the weeds" renders ⲙⲡⲉⲡⲣⲱⲙⲉ ⲕⲁⲁⲩ ⲉϩⲱⲱⲗⲉ ⲙⲡⲍⲓⲍⲁⲛⲓⲟⲛ, with ⲕⲱ (here ⲕⲁⲁⲩ "let them, leave them") plus the infinitive ϩⲱⲱⲗⲉ "pull up, uproot." The same ϩⲱⲱⲗⲉ is kept as "pull up" every time it returns in this logion.
- "In case you go meaning to pull up the weeds and you pull up the wheat along with them" renders ⲙⲏⲡⲱⲥ ⲛⲧⲉⲧⲛⲃⲱⲕ ϫⲉⲉⲧⲉⲧⲛⲛⲁϩⲱⲱⲗⲉ ⲙⲡⲍⲓⲍⲁⲛⲓⲟⲛ ⲛⲧⲉⲧⲛϩⲱⲱⲗⲉ ⲙⲡⲥⲟⲩⲟ ⲛⲙⲙⲁϥ, with the Greek loan ⲙⲏⲡⲱⲥ "lest, in case," ⲃⲱⲕ "go," ϩⲱⲱⲗⲉ repeated, ⲥⲟⲩⲟ "wheat, grain" kept as "wheat," and ⲛⲙⲙⲁϥ "with it / with them." The man's warning is kept as a stated risk, not softened into a direct order on the page.
- "For on the day of harvest the weeds will show plainly; they will be pulled up and burned" renders ϩⲙⲡϩⲟⲟⲩ ⲅⲁⲣ ⲙⲡⲱϩⲥ ⲛⲍⲓⲍⲁⲛⲓⲟⲛ ⲛⲁⲟⲩⲱⲛϩ ⲉⲃⲟⲗ ⲥⲉϩⲟⲗⲟⲩ ⲛⲥⲉⲣⲟⲕϩⲟⲩ, with ϩⲟⲟⲩ "day," the Greek particle ⲅⲁⲣ "for," ⲱϩⲥ "harvest, reaping," ⲟⲩⲱⲛϩ ⲉⲃⲟⲗ "appear, show plainly," ϩⲟⲗⲟⲩ (ϩⲱⲱⲗⲉ again, now "they pull them"), and ⲣⲟⲕϩⲟⲩ "burn them." The harvest clause closes the logion in Coptic order; nothing after it is added.
- G1: checked sentence by sentence against base sentences s0197–s0201; kingdom / enemy-night-sowing / not-let-pull / warning / harvest-appearing-burning order kept; no parallel citations added (PENDING ROBERT).

# Logion 58

- **Witness status:** Coptic-only
- **Parallels:** PENDING ROBERT
- **State:** DRAFTED (G1 self-audit done — see notes)

## Greek

No Greek witness — Coptic alone.

## Coptic (Coptic Scriptorium `thomas.gospel` v6.2.0, CC BY 4.0; base sentence s0202, normalized text as in the base file)

ⲡⲉϫⲉⲓⲏⲥⲟⲩⲥ ϫⲉⲟⲩⲙⲁⲕⲁⲣⲓⲟⲥ ⲡⲉ ⲡⲣⲱⲙⲉ ⲛⲧⲁϥϩⲓⲥⲉ ⲁϥϩⲉ ⲉⲡⲱⲛϩ

## English draft

Jesus said: Blessed is the man who has suffered; he has found life.

## Notes

- "Blessed is the man" renders ⲟⲩⲙⲁⲕⲁⲣⲓⲟⲥ ⲡⲉ ⲡⲣⲱⲙⲉ, with the Greek loan ⲙⲁⲕⲁⲣⲓⲟⲥ "blessed" — the same blessed-word as Logia 49 and 54, kept the same way — and ⲣⲱⲙⲉ "man, person," kept as "man" here as in Logion 57.
- "Who has suffered" renders ⲛⲧⲁϥϩⲓⲥⲉ, with ϩⲓⲥⲉ "trouble, toil, suffering" (a noun used verbally here in the base's tagging). Fork named here, not smoothed on the page: the other honest roads are "has labored / has taken trouble" and "has toiled"; the draft keeps "suffered" as the plainer floor and leaves the toil-road in this note.
- "He has found life" renders ⲁϥϩⲉ ⲉⲡⲱⲛϩ, with ϩⲉ "find" — the same find-word as Logion 56, kept the same way — and ⲱⲛϩ "life, living." No kind of life is specified in the Coptic, and the draft specifies none.
- This is a single-sentence logion (s0202 alone) and is kept as a single sentence; it is not expanded with material from any other blessed-saying in Thomas.
- G1: checked against base sentence s0202; blessed / suffered / found-life order kept; no parallel citations added (PENDING ROBERT).

# Logion 59

- **Witness status:** Coptic-only
- **Parallels:** PENDING ROBERT
- **State:** DRAFTED (G1 self-audit done — see notes)

## Greek

No Greek witness — Coptic alone.

## Coptic (Coptic Scriptorium `thomas.gospel` v6.2.0, CC BY 4.0; base sentences s0203–s0204, normalized text as in the base file)

ⲡⲉϫⲉⲓⲏⲥⲟⲩⲥ ϫⲉϭⲱϣⲧ ⲛⲥⲁⲡⲉⲧⲟⲛϩ ϩⲱⲥ ⲉⲧⲉⲧⲛⲟⲛϩ ϩⲓⲛⲁ ϫⲉⲛⲛⲉⲧⲛⲙⲟⲩ ⲁⲩⲱ ⲛⲧⲉⲧⲛϣⲓⲛⲉ ⲉⲛⲁⲩ ⲉⲣⲟϥ ⲁⲩⲱ ⲧⲉⲧⲛⲛⲁϣϭⲙϭⲟⲙ ⲁⲛ ⲉⲛⲁⲩ

## English draft

Jesus said: Look for the Living One while you are alive, so that you will not die and then seek to see him and not be able to see him.

## Notes

- "Look for the Living One" renders ϭⲱϣⲧ ⲛⲥⲁⲡⲉⲧⲟⲛϩ, with ϭⲱϣⲧ ⲛⲥⲁ "look for, seek after" (the ϭⲱϣⲧ family kept as looking/seeking, as in Logion 51's "waiting for") and ⲡⲉⲧⲟⲛϩ "the one who lives, the Living One," from ⲱⲛϩ "live." Capitalized here as a standing name in the saying; the Coptic itself gives the participle, and the fork ("the living one" lower-case) is named here.
- "While you are alive" renders ϩⲱⲥ ⲉⲧⲉⲧⲛⲟⲛϩ, with the Greek loan ϩⲱⲥ "while, as long as" and ⲟⲛϩ (ⲱⲛϩ) "be alive" with a second-person plural subject — "you" plural, kept plural in the draft.
- "So that you will not die and then seek to see him" renders ϩⲓⲛⲁ ϫⲉⲛⲛⲉⲧⲛⲙⲟⲩ ⲁⲩⲱ ⲛⲧⲉⲧⲛϣⲓⲛⲉ ⲉⲛⲁⲩ ⲉⲣⲟϥ, with the Greek loan ϩⲓⲛⲁ "so that, in order that," the negative purpose ⲛⲛⲉⲧⲛⲙⲟⲩ "you will not die" (ⲙⲟⲩ "die"), ϣⲓⲛⲉ "seek, ask," and ⲉⲛⲁⲩ ⲉⲣⲟϥ "to see him" (ⲛⲁⲩ "see" plus ⲉⲣⲟϥ "to him"). The seeking is kept after the dying in Coptic order; the draft does not move it earlier to make the warning smoother.
- "And not be able to see him" renders ⲁⲩⲱ ⲧⲉⲧⲛⲛⲁϣϭⲙϭⲟⲙ ⲁⲛ ⲉⲛⲁⲩ, with ϣϭⲙϭⲟⲙ "be able" (ϣ- "be able" plus ϭⲙϭⲟⲙ "find strength, be able," as in Logia 55's "will not be able") negated by ⲁⲛ, and a bare ⲉⲛⲁⲩ "to see." The second "him" is carried over from the ⲉⲣⲟϥ just before it; the Coptic's second seeing-clause has no separate object word, and that is stated here rather than hidden.
- G1: checked sentence by sentence against base sentences s0203–s0204; look-for / while-alive / not-die-then-seek / not-able-to-see order kept; no parallel citations added (PENDING ROBERT).

# Logion 60

- **Witness status:** Coptic-only
- **Parallels:** PENDING ROBERT
- **State:** DRAFTED (G1 self-audit done — see notes)

## Greek

No Greek witness — Coptic alone.

## Coptic (Coptic Scriptorium `thomas.gospel` v6.2.0, CC BY 4.0; base sentences s0205–s0210, normalized text as in the base file)

ⲁⲩⲛⲁⲩ ⲉⲟⲩⲥⲁⲙⲁⲣⲉⲓⲧⲏⲥ ⲉϥϥⲓ ⲛⲟⲩϩⲓⲉⲓⲃ · ⲉϥⲃⲏⲕ ⲉϩⲟⲩⲛ ⲉⲧⲓⲟⲩⲇⲁⲓⲁ ⲡⲉϫⲁϥ ⲛⲛⲉϥⲙⲁⲑⲏⲧⲏⲥ ϫⲉⲡⲏ ⲙⲡⲕⲱⲧⲉ ⲙⲡⲉϩⲓⲉⲓⲃ ⲡⲉϫⲁⲩ ⲛⲁϥ ϫⲉⲕⲁⲁⲥ ⲉϥⲛⲁⲙⲟⲟⲩⲧϥ · ⲛϥⲟⲩⲟⲙϥ ⲡⲉϫⲁϥ ⲛⲁⲩ ϩⲱⲥ ⲉϥⲟⲛϩ ϥⲛⲁⲟⲩⲟⲙϥ ⲁⲛ ⲁⲗⲗⲁ ⲉϥϣⲁⲛⲙⲟⲟⲩⲧϥ ⲛϥϣⲱⲡⲉ ⲛⲟⲩⲡⲧⲱⲙⲁ ⲡⲉϫⲁⲩ ϫⲉⲛⲕⲉⲥⲙⲟⲧ ϥⲛⲁϣⲁⲁⲥ ⲁⲛ ⲡⲉϫⲁϥ ⲛⲁⲩ ϫⲉⲛⲧⲱⲧⲛ ϩⲱⲧⲧⲏⲩⲧⲛ ϣⲓⲛⲉ ⲛⲥⲁⲟⲩⲧⲟⲡⲟⲥ ⲛⲏⲧⲛ ⲉϩⲟⲩⲛ ⲉⲩⲁⲛⲁⲡⲁⲩⲥⲓⲥ ϫⲉⲕⲁⲁⲥ ⲛⲛⲉⲧⲛϣⲱⲡⲉ ⲙⲡⲧⲱⲙⲁ ⲛⲥⲉⲟⲩⲟⲙⲧⲏⲩⲧⲛ

## English draft

They saw a Samaritan carrying a lamb, going into Judea. He said to his disciples: That one — what is he doing with the lamb? They said to him: So that he may kill it and eat it. He said to them: While it is alive, he will not eat it; but if he kills it, it will become a corpse. They said: He cannot do it any other way. He said to them: You yourselves, seek a place for yourselves in rest, so that you will not become a corpse and be eaten.

## Notes

- "They saw a Samaritan carrying a lamb, going into Judea" renders ⲁⲩⲛⲁⲩ ⲉⲟⲩⲥⲁⲙⲁⲣⲉⲓⲧⲏⲥ ⲉϥϥⲓ ⲛⲟⲩϩⲓⲉⲓⲃ · ⲉϥⲃⲏⲕ ⲉϩⲟⲩⲛ ⲉⲧⲓⲟⲩⲇⲁⲓⲁ, with ⲛⲁⲩ "see," the Greek loan ⲥⲁⲙⲁⲣⲉⲓⲧⲏⲥ "Samaritan," ϥⲓ "carry" (the same carry-word as Logion 55's cross, kept the same way), ϩⲓⲉⲓⲃ "lamb," ⲃⲏⲕ (ⲃⲱⲕ) "go," ⲉϩⲟⲩⲛ "into, inward," and ⲓⲟⲩⲇⲁⲓⲁ "Judea." The base file's mid-sentence "·" after "lamb" is reproduced in the Coptic block as printed and is not treated as a sentence break in the English. This logion opens without "Jesus said"; the draft does not supply it.
- "That one — what is he doing with the lamb?" renders ⲡⲏ ⲙⲡⲕⲱⲧⲉ ⲙⲡⲉϩⲓⲉⲓⲃ, literally "that one, in the surroundings / around the lamb," with ⲡⲏ "that one (masc.)" and ⲕⲱⲧⲉ "surroundings, what is around." Fork named here, not smoothed away: the Coptic has no verb "do" in this clause; the draft's "what is he doing with" is the plain English floor for an elliptical question, and the more literal road — "that one around the lamb [what about him?]" — is left in this note.
- "So that he may kill it and eat it" renders ϫⲉⲕⲁⲁⲥ ⲉϥⲛⲁⲙⲟⲟⲩⲧϥ · ⲛϥⲟⲩⲟⲙϥ, with ϫⲉⲕⲁⲁⲥ "so that, in order that," ⲙⲟⲟⲩⲧ (ⲙⲟⲩⲟⲩⲧ) "kill," and ⲟⲩⲟⲙ (ⲟⲩⲱⲙ) "eat." The disciples' answer is kept as a stated purpose, in their order — kill first, eat second.
- "While it is alive, he will not eat it; but if he kills it, it will become a corpse" renders ϩⲱⲥ ⲉϥⲟⲛϩ ϥⲛⲁⲟⲩⲟⲙϥ ⲁⲛ ⲁⲗⲗⲁ ⲉϥϣⲁⲛⲙⲟⲟⲩⲧϥ ⲛϥϣⲱⲡⲉ ⲛⲟⲩⲡⲧⲱⲙⲁ, with ϩⲱⲥ "while" (as in Logion 59), ⲟⲛϩ "alive," the negated future ⲟⲩⲟⲙ "eat," the Greek loan ⲁⲗⲗⲁ "but," the conditional ⲉϥϣⲁⲛⲙⲟⲟⲩⲧϥ "if he kills it," ϣⲱⲡⲉ "become," and ⲡⲧⲱⲙⲁ "corpse" — the same corpse-word as Logion 56, kept the same way here and again at this logion's close.
- "He cannot do it any other way" renders ⲛⲕⲉⲥⲙⲟⲧ ϥⲛⲁϣⲁⲁⲥ ⲁⲛ, with ⲕⲉ "other, another," ⲥⲙⲟⲧ "form, way, manner," ϣ- "be able," and ⲁⲁⲥ (ⲉⲓⲣⲉ) "do it" negated by ⲁⲛ. Fork: the more literal road is "in another form/way he will not be able to do it"; the draft keeps the plain floor and names the form-word here.
- "You yourselves, seek a place for yourselves in rest, so that you will not become a corpse and be eaten" renders ⲛⲧⲱⲧⲛ ϩⲱⲧⲧⲏⲩⲧⲛ ϣⲓⲛⲉ ⲛⲥⲁⲟⲩⲧⲟⲡⲟⲥ ⲛⲏⲧⲛ ⲉϩⲟⲩⲛ ⲉⲩⲁⲛⲁⲡⲁⲩⲥⲓⲥ ϫⲉⲕⲁⲁⲥ ⲛⲛⲉⲧⲛϣⲱⲡⲉ ⲙⲡⲧⲱⲙⲁ ⲛⲥⲉⲟⲩⲟⲙⲧⲏⲩⲧⲛ, with the emphatic ⲛⲧⲱⲧⲛ ϩⲱⲧⲧⲏⲩⲧⲛ "you yourselves," ϣⲓⲛⲉ ⲛⲥⲁ "seek after" (as in Logion 59), the Greek loans ⲧⲟⲡⲟⲥ "place" and ⲁⲛⲁⲡⲁⲩⲥⲓⲥ "rest" — the same rest-word as Logia 50 and 51, kept the same way — and the closing ⲡⲧⲱⲙⲁ "corpse" plus ⲟⲩⲟⲙ "eat" in the passive sense "be eaten." The seek-command and the not-become-a-corpse purpose are kept in Coptic order; no moral is added after them.
- G1: checked sentence by sentence against base sentences s0205–s0210; Samaritan scene / lamb question / kill-and-eat answer / alive-not-eaten, killed-becomes-corpse / no-other-way / seek-a-place-in-rest order kept; no parallel citations added (PENDING ROBERT).

# Logion 61

- **Witness status:** Coptic-only
- **Parallels:** PENDING ROBERT
- **State:** DRAFTED (G1 self-audit done — see notes)

## Greek

No Greek witness — Coptic alone.

## Coptic (Coptic Scriptorium `thomas.gospel` v6.2.0, CC BY 4.0; base sentences s0211–s0218, normalized text as in the base file)

ⲡⲉϫⲉⲓⲏⲥⲟⲩⲥ ⲟⲩⲛⲥⲛⲁⲩ ⲛⲁⲙⲧⲟⲛ ⲙⲙⲟⲩ ϩⲓⲟⲩϭⲗⲟϭ ⲡⲟⲩⲁ ⲛⲁⲙⲟⲩ ⲡⲟⲩⲁ ⲛⲁⲱⲛϩ ⲡⲉϫⲉⲥⲁⲗⲱⲙⲏ ⲛⲧⲕⲛⲓⲙ ⲡⲣⲱⲙⲉ ϩⲱⲥ ⲉⲃⲟⲗ ϩⲛⲟⲩⲁ ⲁⲕⲧⲉⲗⲟ ⲉϫⲙⲡⲁϭⲗⲟϭ ⲁⲩⲱ ⲁⲕⲟⲩⲱⲙ ⲉⲃⲟⲗ ϩⲛⲧⲁⲧⲣⲁⲡⲉⲍⲁ ⲡⲉϫⲉⲓⲏⲥⲟⲩⲥ ⲛⲁⲥ ϫⲉⲁⲛⲟⲕ ⲡⲉ ⲡⲉⲧϣⲟⲟⲡ ⲉⲃⲟⲗ ϩⲙⲡⲉⲧϣⲏϣ ⲁⲩϯ ⲛⲁⲓ ⲉⲃⲟⲗ ϩⲛⲛⲁⲡⲁⲉⲓⲱⲧ ⲁⲛⲟⲕⲧⲉⲕⲙⲁⲑⲏⲧⲏⲥ ⲉⲧⲃⲉⲡⲁⲓ ϯϫⲱ ⲙⲙⲟⲥ ϫⲉϩⲟⲧⲁⲛ ⲉϥϣⲁⲛϣⲱⲡⲉ ⲉϥϣⲏϥ ϥⲛⲁⲙⲟⲩϩⲟⲩⲟⲉⲓⲛ ϩⲟⲧⲁⲛ ⲇⲉ ⲉϥϣⲁⲛϣⲱⲡⲉ ⲉϥⲡⲏϣ ϥⲛⲁⲙⲟⲩϩ ⲛⲕⲁⲕⲉ

## English draft

Jesus said: Two will rest on a bed. One will die, and one will live. Salome said: Who are you, man, that, as one coming from a single one, you climbed onto my bed and ate from my table? Jesus said to her: I am the one who is from the one who is whole and even. What belongs to my Father has been given to me. I am your disciple. For this reason I say: when he is whole, he will be filled with light; but when he is divided, he will be filled with darkness.

## Notes

- "Two will rest on a bed. One will die, and one will live" renders ⲟⲩⲛⲥⲛⲁⲩ ⲛⲁⲙⲧⲟⲛ ⲙⲙⲟⲩ ϩⲓⲟⲩϭⲗⲟϭ ⲡⲟⲩⲁ ⲛⲁⲙⲟⲩ ⲡⲟⲩⲁ ⲛⲁⲱⲛϩ, with ⲥⲛⲁⲩ "two," the future ⲛⲁ- plus ⲙⲧⲟⲛ "rest" (the rest-word family kept with the resting sayings earlier in this draft), ϭⲗⲟϭ "bed, couch," and the paired ⲟⲩⲁ … ⲟⲩⲁ "one … one" with ⲙⲟⲩ "die" and ⲱⲛϩ "live." The saying opens the logion without a bed-owner or a reason; none is supplied.
- "Who are you, man, that, as one coming from a single one, you climbed onto my bed and ate from my table?" renders ⲛⲧⲕⲛⲓⲙ ⲡⲣⲱⲙⲉ ϩⲱⲥ ⲉⲃⲟⲗ ϩⲛⲟⲩⲁ ⲁⲕⲧⲉⲗⲟ ⲉϫⲙⲡⲁϭⲗⲟϭ ⲁⲩⲱ ⲁⲕⲟⲩⲱⲙ ⲉⲃⲟⲗ ϩⲛⲧⲁⲧⲣⲁⲡⲉⲍⲁ, with ⲛⲓⲙ "who," ⲣⲱⲙⲉ "man, person," ⲧⲉⲗⲟ "climb up, mount," ϭⲗⲟϭ "bed" again, ⲟⲩⲱⲙ "eat," and the Greek loan ⲧⲣⲁⲡⲉⲍⲁ "table." Fork named here, not smoothed away: ϩⲱⲥ ⲉⲃⲟⲗ ϩⲛⲟⲩⲁ is literally "as [one] out of / from one" — no noun stands after "one" in the Coptic; the draft's "a single one" keeps that bareness, and the more interpretive roads ("as a son of the One," "as one sent from the One") are left in this note only.
- "I am the one who is from the one who is whole and even" renders ⲁⲛⲟⲕ ⲡⲉ ⲡⲉⲧϣⲟⲟⲡ ⲉⲃⲟⲗ ϩⲙⲡⲉⲧϣⲏϣ, with ⲁⲛⲟⲕ ⲡⲉ "I am (the one)," ϣⲟⲟⲡ "be, exist," and ϣⲏϣ (lemma ϣⲱϣ) "be equal, even, undivided." Fork: the pair "whole and even" in the draft is the plain floor for a word whose range runs "equal / level / whole / undivided"; no single English word carries all of it, and the choice is flagged here rather than hidden in the text. The answer answers Salome's "from one" in kind and does not expand it.
- "What belongs to my Father has been given to me" renders ⲁⲩϯ ⲛⲁⲓ ⲉⲃⲟⲗ ϩⲛⲛⲁⲡⲁⲉⲓⲱⲧ, with ϯ "give," ⲛⲁⲓ "to me," and ⲉⲃⲟⲗ ϩⲛ ⲛⲁ-ⲡⲁ-ⲉⲓⲱⲧ "out of the (things) of my Father." The Coptic has no word "things" or "what belongs"; the draft's "what belongs to my Father" is the plain floor for the bare possessive plural, named here.
- "I am your disciple" renders ⲁⲛⲟⲕⲧⲉⲕⲙⲁⲑⲏⲧⲏⲥ, with the Greek loan ⲙⲁⲑⲏⲧⲏⲥ "disciple" kept as throughout this draft. The speaker is Salome by the speech order of the Coptic; the base file gives no new "she said" before this line, and the draft follows the exchange order without adding one.
- "When he is whole, he will be filled with light; but when he is divided, he will be filled with darkness" renders ϩⲟⲧⲁⲛ ⲉϥϣⲁⲛϣⲱⲡⲉ ⲉϥϣⲏϥ ϥⲛⲁⲙⲟⲩϩⲟⲩⲟⲉⲓⲛ ϩⲟⲧⲁⲛ ⲇⲉ ⲉϥϣⲁⲛϣⲱⲡⲉ ⲉϥⲡⲏϣ ϥⲛⲁⲙⲟⲩϩ ⲛⲕⲁⲕⲉ, with ϩⲟⲧⲁⲛ "when," ⲙⲟⲩϩ "fill, be filled," ⲟⲩⲟⲉⲓⲛ "light" and ⲕⲁⲕⲉ "darkness" — the same light-word as Logia 24 and 50, kept the same way — and the opposed statives ⲉϥϣⲏϥ (lemma ϣⲱϥ in the base file, paired here against "divided") and ⲉϥⲡⲏϣ (lemma ⲡⲱϣ) "divided, split." Fork: the first stative's base lemma is uncertain in the edition; the draft renders it "whole" from the pair's own opposition (whole against divided, light against darkness) and names that here rather than presenting it as settled. The "he" has no named owner in the Coptic; none is supplied.
- G1: checked sentence by sentence against base sentences s0211–s0218; two-on-a-bed / die-live / Salome's question / from-the-whole-one answer / Father's-gift / disciple reply / whole-light, divided-darkness order kept; no parallel citations added (PENDING ROBERT).

# Logion 62

- **Witness status:** Coptic-only
- **Parallels:** PENDING ROBERT
- **State:** DRAFTED (G1 self-audit done — see notes)

## Greek

No Greek witness — Coptic alone.

## Coptic (Coptic Scriptorium `thomas.gospel` v6.2.0, CC BY 4.0; base sentences s0219–s0220, normalized text as in the base file)

ⲡⲉϫⲉⲓⲏⲥⲟⲩⲥ ϫⲉⲉⲓϫⲱ ⲛⲛⲁⲙⲩⲥⲧⲏⲣⲓⲟⲛ ⲛⲛⲉⲧⲙⲡϣⲁ ⲛⲛⲁⲙⲩⲥⲧⲏⲣⲓⲟⲛ ⲡⲉⲧⲉⲧⲉⲕⲟⲩⲛⲁⲙ ⲛⲁⲁⲁϥ ⲙⲡⲣⲧⲣⲉⲧⲉⲕϩⲃⲟⲩⲣ ⲉⲓⲙⲉ ϫⲉⲉⲥⲣⲟⲩ

## English draft

Jesus said: I tell my mysteries to those who are worthy of my mysteries. What your right hand will do, do not let your left hand know what it is doing.

## Notes

- "I tell my mysteries to those who are worthy of my mysteries" renders ⲉⲓϫⲱ ⲛⲛⲁⲙⲩⲥⲧⲏⲣⲓⲟⲛ ⲛⲛⲉⲧⲙⲡϣⲁ ⲛⲛⲁⲙⲩⲥⲧⲏⲣⲓⲟⲛ, with ϫⲱ "say, tell," the Greek loan ⲙⲩⲥⲧⲏⲣⲓⲟⲛ "mystery, secret" kept as "mysteries" in both places, and ⲙⲡϣⲁ "be worthy" — the same worthy-word as Logion 56. The repetition (mysteries … worthy of my mysteries) is the Coptic's own and is kept, not varied away.
- "What your right hand will do, do not let your left hand know what it is doing" renders ⲡⲉⲧⲉⲧⲉⲕⲟⲩⲛⲁⲙ ⲛⲁⲁⲁϥ ⲙⲡⲣⲧⲣⲉⲧⲉⲕϩⲃⲟⲩⲣ ⲉⲓⲙⲉ ϫⲉⲉⲥⲣⲟⲩ, with ⲟⲩⲛⲁⲙ "right (side, hand)," the future ⲛⲁⲁⲁϥ "will do it" (ⲉⲓⲣⲉ "do, make"), the negative causative ⲙⲡⲣⲧⲣⲉ- "do not let, do not cause," ϩⲃⲟⲩⲣ "left (side, hand)," and ⲉⲓⲙⲉ "know." Fork named here: the Coptic names only the "right" and the "left" — the word "hand" is not written in either place; the draft's "right hand / left hand" is the plain English floor for the pair, and the more literal road ("what your right will do, do not let your left know") is left in this note. The closing ϫⲉⲉⲥⲣⲟⲩ is literally "that it does what" — the draft's "what it is doing" keeps that clause in order without importing a new object.
- No doublet stands in this logion to merge; the secrets-giving and the hand pair are kept as two sayings-units inside one logion, as the base file's chapter division gives them.
- G1: checked sentence by sentence against base sentences s0219–s0220; mysteries-to-the-worthy / right-do, left-not-know order kept; no parallel citations added (PENDING ROBERT).

# Logion 63

- **Witness status:** Coptic-only
- **Parallels:** PENDING ROBERT
- **State:** DRAFTED (G1 self-audit done — see notes)

## Greek

No Greek witness — Coptic alone.

## Coptic (Coptic Scriptorium `thomas.gospel` v6.2.0, CC BY 4.0; base sentences s0221–s0225, normalized text as in the base file)

ⲡⲉϫⲉⲓⲏⲥⲟⲩⲥ ϫⲉⲛⲉⲩⲛⲟⲩⲣⲱⲙⲉ ⲙⲡⲗⲟⲩⲥⲓⲟⲥ ⲉⲩⲛⲧⲁϥ ⲙⲙⲁⲩ ⲛϩⲁϩ ⲛⲭⲣⲏⲙⲁ ⲡⲉϫⲁϥ ϫⲉϯⲛⲁⲣⲭⲣⲱ ⲛⲛⲁⲭⲣⲏⲙⲁ ϫⲉⲕⲁⲁⲥ ⲉⲓⲛⲁϫⲟ ⲛⲧⲁⲱϩⲥ ⲛⲧⲁⲧⲱϭⲉ ⲛⲧⲁⲙⲟⲩϩ ⲛⲛⲁⲉϩⲱⲣ ⲛⲕⲁⲣⲡⲟⲥ ϩⲓⲛⲁ ϫⲉⲛⲛⲁⲣϭⲣⲱϩ ⲛⲗⲁⲁⲩ ⲛⲁⲓ ⲛⲉ ⲛⲉϥⲙⲉⲉⲩⲉ ⲉⲣⲟⲟⲩ ϩⲙⲡⲉϥϩⲏⲧ ⲁⲩⲱ ϩⲛⲧⲟⲩϣⲏ ⲉⲧⲙⲙⲁⲩ ⲁϥⲙⲟⲩ ⲡⲉⲧⲉⲟⲩⲙⲙⲁϫⲉ ⲙⲙⲟϥ ⲙⲁⲣⲉϥⲥⲱⲧⲙ

## English draft

Jesus said: There was a rich man who had much money. He said: I will use my money, so that I may sow and reap and plant and fill my storehouses with fruit, so that I will lack nothing. These were the things he was thinking in his heart. And that night he died. Whoever has ears, let him hear.

## Notes

- "There was a rich man who had much money" renders ⲛⲉⲩⲛⲟⲩⲣⲱⲙⲉ ⲙⲡⲗⲟⲩⲥⲓⲟⲥ ⲉⲩⲛⲧⲁϥ ⲙⲙⲁⲩ ⲛϩⲁϩ ⲛⲭⲣⲏⲙⲁ, with the preterite existence frame ⲛⲉⲩⲛ- "there was," ⲣⲱⲙⲉ "man," the Greek loan ⲡⲗⲟⲩⲥⲓⲟⲥ "rich (man)," ϩⲁϩ "much, many," and the Greek loan ⲭⲣⲏⲙⲁ "money, property, goods." The draft keeps "money" for ⲭⲣⲏⲙⲁ here and in the man's speech; the wider road ("wealth, possessions") is named here — the word's range is money-to-property, and no narrower coin-word is written in the Coptic.
- "I will use my money, so that I may sow and reap and plant and fill my storehouses with fruit, so that I will lack nothing" renders ϯⲛⲁⲣⲭⲣⲱ ⲛⲛⲁⲭⲣⲏⲙⲁ ϫⲉⲕⲁⲁⲥ ⲉⲓⲛⲁϫⲟ ⲛⲧⲁⲱϩⲥ ⲛⲧⲁⲧⲱϭⲉ ⲛⲧⲁⲙⲟⲩϩ ⲛⲛⲁⲉϩⲱⲣ ⲛⲕⲁⲣⲡⲟⲥ ϩⲓⲛⲁ ϫⲉⲛⲛⲁⲣϭⲣⲱϩ ⲛⲗⲁⲁⲩ, with ⲣⲭⲣⲱ "make use of, employ" (Greek chrō family, kept plain as "use"), the chain of purpose-verbs ϫⲟ "sow," ⲱϩⲥ "reap, harvest," ⲧⲱϭⲉ (ⲧⲱⲱϭⲉ) "plant," and ⲙⲟⲩϩ "fill" — the same fill-word as Logion 61, kept the same way — ⲉϩⲱⲣ (plural of ⲁϩⲟ) "storehouses, treasuries," the Greek loan ⲕⲁⲣⲡⲟⲥ "fruit, produce," and the closing ⲛⲛⲁⲣϭⲣⲱϩ ⲛⲗⲁⲁⲩ "I will not lack anything," with ϭⲣⲱϩ "lack, be in need" and ⲗⲁⲁⲩ "anything, anyone." The farm order (sow, reap, plant) is the Coptic's own sequence and is kept as given, not re-sorted into sowing-planting-reaping logic.
- "These were the things he was thinking in his heart" renders ⲛⲁⲓ ⲛⲉ ⲛⲉϥⲙⲉⲉⲩⲉ ⲉⲣⲟⲟⲩ ϩⲙⲡⲉϥϩⲏⲧ, with ⲛⲁⲓ "these," ⲙⲉⲉⲩⲉ "think, thoughts," and ϩⲏⲧ "heart." The Coptic says "these were what he was thinking"; the draft's "the things he was thinking" is the plain floor, named here.
- "And that night he died" renders ⲁⲩⲱ ϩⲛⲧⲟⲩϣⲏ ⲉⲧⲙⲙⲁⲩ ⲁϥⲙⲟⲩ, with ⲟⲩϣⲏ "night," ⲉⲧⲙⲙⲁⲩ "that (one)," and ⲙⲟⲩ "die" — the same die-word as Logion 61's one-will-die, kept the same way. No cause, no voice from heaven, and no moral is written in the Coptic; none is added.
- "Whoever has ears, let him hear" renders ⲡⲉⲧⲉⲟⲩⲙⲙⲁϫⲉ ⲙⲙⲟϥ ⲙⲁⲣⲉϥⲥⲱⲧⲙ, with ⲙⲁϫⲉ "ear" (the same ear-word as Logion 33, kept the same way), ⲙⲁⲣⲉϥ- "let him," and ⲥⲱⲧⲙ "hear, listen." The formula closes this logion and returns at Logion 65; it is rendered identically in both places and each occurrence stands on its own worksheet.
- G1: checked sentence by sentence against base sentences s0221–s0225; rich-man / use-money plan / heart-thoughts / that-night death / ears formula order kept; no parallel citations added (PENDING ROBERT).

# Logion 64

- **Witness status:** Coptic-only
- **Parallels:** PENDING ROBERT
- **State:** DRAFTED (G1 self-audit done — see notes)

## Greek

No Greek witness — Coptic alone.

## Coptic (Coptic Scriptorium `thomas.gospel` v6.2.0, CC BY 4.0; base sentences s0226–s0253, normalized text as in the base file)

ⲡⲉϫⲉⲓⲏⲥⲟⲩⲥ ϫⲉⲟⲩⲣⲱⲙⲉ ⲛⲉⲩⲛⲧⲁϥ ϩⲉⲛϣⲙⲙⲟ ⲁⲩⲱ ⲛⲧⲁⲣⲉϥⲥⲟⲃⲧⲉ ⲙⲡⲇⲉⲓⲡⲛⲟⲛ ⲁϥϫⲟⲟⲩ ⲙⲡⲉϥϩⲙϩⲁⲗ ϩⲓⲛⲁ ⲉϥⲛⲁⲧⲱϩⲙ ⲛⲛϣⲙⲙⲟⲓ ⲁϥⲃⲱⲕ ⲙⲡϣⲟⲣⲡ · ⲡⲉϫⲁϥ ⲛⲁϥ ϫⲉⲡⲁϫⲟⲉⲓⲥ ⲧⲱϩⲙ ⲙⲙⲟⲕ ⲡⲉϫⲁϥ ϫⲉⲟⲩⲛⲧⲁⲓ ϩⲉⲛϩⲟⲙⲧ ⲉϩⲉⲛⲉⲙⲡⲟⲣⲟⲥ ⲥⲉⲛⲏⲩ ϣⲁⲣⲟⲓ ⲉⲣⲟⲩϩⲉ ϯⲛⲁⲃⲱⲕ ⲛⲧⲁⲟⲩⲉϩⲥⲁϩⲛⲉ ⲛⲁⲩ ϯⲣⲡⲁⲣⲁⲓⲧⲉⲓ ⲙⲡⲇⲉⲓⲡⲛⲟⲛ ⲁϥⲃⲱⲕ ϣⲁⲕⲉⲟⲩⲁ ⲡⲉϫⲁϥ ⲛⲁϥ ϫⲉⲁⲡⲁϫⲟⲉⲓⲥ ⲧⲱϩⲙ ⲙⲙⲟⲕ ⲡⲉϫⲁϥ ⲛⲁϥ ϫⲉⲁⲓⲧⲟⲟⲩⲟⲩⲏⲓ ⲁⲩⲱ ⲥⲉⲣⲁⲓⲧⲉⲓ ⲙⲙⲟⲓ ⲛⲟⲩϩⲏⲙⲉⲣⲁ ϯⲛⲁⲥⲣϥⲉ ⲁⲛ ⲁϥⲉⲓ ϣⲁⲕⲉⲟⲩⲁ ⲡⲉϫⲁϥ ⲛⲁϥ ϫⲉⲡⲁϫⲟⲉⲓⲥ ⲧⲱϩⲙ ⲙⲙⲟⲕ ⲡⲉϫⲁϥ ⲛⲁϥ ϫⲉⲡⲁϣⲃⲏⲣ ⲛⲁⲣϣⲉⲗⲉⲉⲧ ⲁⲩⲱ ⲁⲛⲟⲕ ⲉⲧⲛⲁⲣⲇⲓⲡⲛⲟⲛ ϯⲛⲁϣⲉⲓ ⲁⲛ ϯⲣⲡⲁⲣⲁⲓⲧⲉⲓ ⲙⲡⲇⲉⲓⲡⲛⲟⲛ ⲁϥⲃⲱⲕ ϣⲁⲕⲉⲟⲩⲁ ⲡⲉϫⲁϥ ⲛⲁϥ ϫⲉⲡⲁϫⲟⲉⲓⲥ ⲧⲱϩⲙ ⲙⲙⲟⲕ ⲡⲉϫⲁϥ ⲛⲁϥ ϫⲉⲁⲓⲧⲟⲟⲩ ⲛⲟⲩⲕⲱⲙⲏ ⲉⲓⲃⲏⲕ ⲉϫⲓⲛϣⲱⲙ ϯⲛⲁϣⲉⲓ ⲁⲛ ϯⲣⲡⲁⲣⲁⲓⲧⲉⲓ ⲁϥⲉⲓ ⲛϭⲓⲡϩⲙϩⲁⲗ ⲁϥϫⲟⲟⲥ ⲉⲡⲉϥϫⲟⲉⲓⲥ ϫⲉⲛⲉⲛⲧⲁⲕⲧⲁϩⲙⲟⲩ ⲉⲡⲇⲉⲓⲡⲛⲟⲛ ⲁⲩⲡⲁⲣⲁⲓⲧⲉⲓ ⲡⲉϫⲉⲡϫⲟⲉⲓⲥ ⲙⲡⲉϥϩⲙϩⲁⲗ ϫⲉⲃⲱⲕ ⲉⲡⲥⲁ ⲛⲃⲟⲗ ⲉⲛϩⲓⲟⲟⲩⲉ ⲛⲉⲧⲕⲛⲁϩⲉ ⲉⲣⲟⲟⲩ ⲁⲛⲓⲥⲟⲩ ϫⲉⲕⲁⲁⲥ ⲉⲩⲛⲁⲣⲇⲓⲡⲛⲉⲓ ⲛⲣⲉϥⲧⲟⲟⲩ ⲙⲛⲛⲉϣⲟⲧⲉ ⲥⲉⲛⲁⲃⲱⲕ ⲁⲛ ⲉϩⲟⲩⲛ ⲉⲛⲧⲟⲡⲟⲥ ⲙⲡⲁⲉⲓⲱⲧ

## English draft

Jesus said: A man had guests. And when he had prepared the dinner, he sent his servant so that he might invite the guests. He went to the first. He said to him: My master invites you. He said: I have money with merchants. They are coming to me in the evening. I will go and give them orders. I excuse myself from the dinner. He went to another. He said to him: My master invites you. He said to him: I have bought a house, and they need me for a day. I will have no free time. He came to another. He said to him: My master invites you. He said to him: My friend will have a wedding, and I am the one who will prepare the dinner. I will not be able to come. I excuse myself from the dinner. He went to another. He said to him: My master invites you. He said to him: I have bought a village. I am going to collect the rent. I will not be able to come. I excuse myself. The servant came. He told his master: Those whom you invited to the dinner have excused themselves. The master said to his servant: Go outside to the roads. Whoever you find, bring them, so that they may dine. Buyers and merchants will not go into the places of my Father.

## Notes

- "A man had guests" renders ⲟⲩⲣⲱⲙⲉ ⲛⲉⲩⲛⲧⲁϥ ϩⲉⲛϣⲙⲙⲟ, with ⲣⲱⲙⲉ "man" and ϣⲙⲙⲟ "strangers, guests, sojourners." Fork: ⲣⲱⲙⲉ is not the "master/lord" word; the draft keeps "a man" at the opening and lets ⲡⲁϫⲟⲉⲓⲥ / ⲡϫⲟⲉⲓⲥ "my master / the master" (ϫⲟⲉⲓⲥ, Greek kyrios family in use) carry the master-language where the Coptic itself uses it, in the servant's mouth and at the close. That distinction is the Coptic's and is kept.
- The dinner frame — "when he had prepared the dinner, he sent his servant so that he might invite the guests" — renders ⲛⲧⲁⲣⲉϥⲥⲟⲃⲧⲉ ⲙⲡⲇⲉⲓⲡⲛⲟⲛ ⲁϥϫⲟⲟⲩ ⲙⲡⲉϥϩⲙϩⲁⲗ ϩⲓⲛⲁ ⲉϥⲛⲁⲧⲱϩⲙ ⲛⲛϣⲙⲙⲟⲓ, with ⲥⲟⲃⲧⲉ "prepare, make ready," the Greek loan ⲇⲉⲓⲡⲛⲟⲛ "dinner, supper" kept as "dinner" throughout (including the servant's own dinner-preparing below, where the same word stands), ϩⲙϩⲁⲗ "servant," and ⲧⲱϩⲙ "invite, call in" — the invite-word used at every door in this logion, kept the same way each time.
- The four refusals are kept in Coptic order and not merged or re-sorted: (1) money with merchants; (2) house bought; (3) friend's wedding; (4) village bought. Each ends in its own excuse-formula where the Coptic gives one; the repetitions of ⲣⲡⲁⲣⲁⲓⲧⲉⲓ / ⲡⲁⲣⲁⲓⲧⲉⲓ (Greek paraitei, "excuse oneself, beg off") are kept as "I excuse myself" — the first man's "I excuse myself from the dinner," the wedding-man's and the village-man's own formulas, and the house-man's refusal, which in the Coptic carries no separate excuse-formula and closes instead with "I will have no free time," kept as written — and the master's summary ⲁⲩⲡⲁⲣⲁⲓⲧⲉⲓ "they excused themselves" uses the same word. The draft does not soften these into one stock phrase in English; the sameness is the point and stays visible.
- First refusal forks, named here: "I have money with merchants" renders ⲟⲩⲛⲧⲁⲓ ϩⲉⲛϩⲟⲙⲧ ⲉϩⲉⲛⲉⲙⲡⲟⲣⲟⲥ, with ϩⲟⲙⲧ "bronze, copper, money" and the Greek loan ⲉⲙⲡⲟⲣⲟⲥ "merchants, traders." The Coptic states only that he has money in relation to merchants (ⲉ- "with, toward"); whether they owe him, hold his, or are owed by him is not written, and the draft's bare "with" leaves that open. "They are coming to me in the evening" is ⲥⲉⲛⲏⲩ ϣⲁⲣⲟⲓ ⲉⲣⲟⲩϩⲉ, with ⲣⲟⲩϩⲉ "evening." "I will go and give them orders" is ⲟⲩⲉϩⲥⲁϩⲛⲉ "give orders, command" — an order-giving word, not an invitation word.
- Second refusal forks: "I have bought a house" is ⲁⲓⲧⲟⲟⲩⲟⲩⲏⲓ, with ⲧⲟⲟⲩ "buy" and ⲏⲓ "house." "They need me for a day" renders ⲥⲉⲣⲁⲓⲧⲉⲓ ⲙⲙⲟⲓ ⲛⲟⲩϩⲏⲙⲉⲣⲁ, with the Greek loan ϩⲏⲙⲉⲣⲁ "day." Fork: the verb here (ⲣⲁⲓⲧⲉⲓ family in the base file's lemma) is obscure beside the clear excuse-verb elsewhere; the draft's "they need me" is the plain floor for "they [call on / require] me for a day," and the more literal road is left in this note. "I will have no free time" is ϯⲛⲁⲥⲣϥⲉ ⲁⲛ, with ⲥⲣϥⲉ "be at leisure, have free time" negated.
- Third refusal: "My friend will have a wedding" renders ⲡⲁϣⲃⲏⲣ ⲛⲁⲣϣⲉⲗⲉⲉⲧ, with ϣⲃⲏⲣ "friend, companion" and ⲣϣⲉⲗⲉⲉⲧ "make a wedding, marry." "I am the one who will prepare the dinner" is ⲁⲛⲟⲕ ⲉⲧⲛⲁⲣⲇⲓⲡⲛⲟⲛ — literally "I am the one who will make dinner," using the same ⲇⲉⲓⲡⲛⲟⲛ word as the master's dinner. "I will not be able to come" renders ϯⲛⲁϣⲉⲓ ⲁⲛ, where the base file parses ϣ- as a noun token; the draft reads the pair as "be able + come" by the sentence's own shape (he cannot come, then excuses himself) and flags that reading here rather than in the text.
- Fourth refusal: "I have bought a village" is ⲁⲓⲧⲟⲟⲩ ⲛⲟⲩⲕⲱⲙⲏ, with the Greek loan ⲕⲱⲙⲏ "village." Fork: "I am going to collect the rent" renders ⲉⲓⲃⲏⲕ ⲉϫⲓⲛϣⲱⲙ, with ⲃⲏⲕ (ⲃⲱⲕ) "going" and ϣⲱⲙ "rent, tax, tribute-payment" in the base file's lemma. The draft's "collect the rent" keeps the payment-word concrete; whether it is rent from the village or a tax-gathering errand is not spelled in the Coptic and is left open here.
- "Go outside to the roads. Whoever you find, bring them, so that they may dine" renders the master's closing order (s0251–s0252), with ⲃⲱⲕ "go," ⲡⲥⲁ ⲛⲃⲟⲗ "the outside," ϩⲓⲟⲟⲩⲉ (ϩⲓⲏ) "roads," ϩⲉ "find," and ⲁⲛⲓⲥⲟⲩ "bring them." Sentence-split note: the base file breaks its sentence after ⲣⲇⲓⲡⲛⲉⲓ "will dine," leaving ⲛⲣⲉϥⲧⲟⲟⲩ "buyers" at the end of s0252 with its verb in s0253. The draft follows the sense across that break: "Buyers and merchants will not go into the places of my Father" renders ⲛⲣⲉϥⲧⲟⲟⲩ ⲙⲛⲛⲉϣⲟⲧⲉ ⲥⲉⲛⲁⲃⲱⲕ ⲁⲛ ⲉϩⲟⲩⲛ ⲉⲛⲧⲟⲡⲟⲥ ⲙⲡⲁⲉⲓⲱⲧ, with ⲣⲉϥⲧⲟⲟⲩ "buyers," ϣⲟⲧⲉ "merchants," the Greek loan ⲧⲟⲡⲟⲥ "places" (the same place-word as Logia 24 and 60, kept the same way), and ⲡⲁⲉⲓⲱⲧ "my Father." "My Father" at the very close is the Coptic's own shift of speaker-reference; the draft does not harmonize it back to "the master."
- G1: checked sentence by sentence against base sentences s0226–s0253; guests / dinner-prepared / four doors in order (merchants, house, wedding, village) with each excuse kept / servant's report / go-to-the-roads order / buyers-and-merchants close kept; no parallel citations added (PENDING ROBERT).

Logion 65  (source worksheet: logion-065.md)

> DRAFT — working translation from the Coptic (and Greek where it survives), prepared with AI assistance; not reviewed by a Coptic scholar; not finished. Corrections invited and credited.

# Logion 65

- **Witness status:** Coptic-only
- **Parallels:** PENDING ROBERT
- **State:** DRAFTED (G1 self-audit done — see notes)

## Greek

No Greek witness — Coptic alone.

## Coptic (Coptic Scriptorium `thomas.gospel` v6.2.0, CC BY 4.0; base sentences s0254–s0265, normalized text as in the base file)

ⲡⲉϫⲁϥ ϫⲉⲟⲩⲣⲱⲙⲉ ⲛⲭⲣⲏⲥⲧⲟⲥ ⲛⲉⲟⲩⲛⲧⲁϥ ⲛⲟⲩⲙⲁ ⲛⲉⲗⲟⲟⲗⲉ ⲁϥⲧⲁⲁϥ ⲛϩⲉⲛⲟⲩⲟⲉⲓⲉ ϩⲓⲛⲁ ⲉⲩⲛⲁⲣϩⲱⲃ ⲉⲣⲟϥ ⲛϥϫⲓ ⲙⲡⲉϥⲕⲁⲣⲡⲟⲥ ⲛⲧⲟⲟⲧⲟⲩ ⲁϥϫⲟⲟⲩ ⲙⲡⲉϥϩⲙϩⲁⲗ ϫⲉⲕⲁⲁⲥ ⲉⲣⲉⲛⲟⲩⲟⲉⲓⲉ ⲛⲁϯ ⲛⲁϥ ⲙⲡⲕⲁⲣⲡⲟⲥ ⲙⲡⲙⲁ ⲛⲉⲗⲟⲟⲗⲉ ⲁⲩⲉⲙⲁϩⲧⲉ ⲙⲡⲉϥϩⲙϩⲁⲗ ⲁⲩϩⲓⲟⲩⲉ ⲉⲣⲟϥ ⲛⲉⲕⲉⲕⲟⲩⲓ ⲡⲉ ⲛⲥⲉⲙⲟⲟⲩⲧϥ ⲁⲡϩⲙϩⲁⲗ ⲃⲱⲕ ⲁϥϫⲟⲟⲥ ⲉⲡⲉϥϫⲟⲉⲓⲥ ⲡⲉϫⲉⲡⲉϥϫⲟⲉⲓⲥ ϫⲉⲙⲉϣⲁⲕ ⲙⲡⲉϥⲥⲟⲩⲱⲛⲟⲩ ⲁϥϫⲟⲟⲩ ⲛⲕⲉϩⲙϩⲁⲗ ⲁⲛⲟⲩⲟⲉⲓⲉ ϩⲓⲟⲩⲉ ⲉⲡⲕⲉⲟⲩⲁ ⲧⲟⲧⲉ ⲁⲡϫⲟⲉⲓⲥ ϫⲟⲟⲩ ⲙⲡⲉϥϣⲏⲣⲉ ⲡⲉϫⲁϥ ϫⲉⲙⲉϣⲁⲕ ⲥⲉⲛⲁϣⲓⲡⲉ ϩⲏⲧϥ ⲙⲡⲁϣⲏⲣⲉ ⲁⲛⲟⲩⲟⲉⲓⲉ ⲉⲧⲙⲙⲁⲩ ⲉⲡⲉⲓⲥⲉⲥⲟⲟⲩⲛ ϫⲉⲛⲧⲟϥ ⲡⲉ ⲡⲉⲕⲗⲏⲣⲟⲛⲟⲙⲟⲥ ⲙⲡⲙⲁ ⲛⲉⲗⲟⲟⲗⲉ ⲁⲩϭⲟⲡϥ ⲁⲩⲙⲟⲟⲩⲧϥ ⲡⲉⲧⲉⲩⲙⲙⲁⲁϫⲉ ⲙⲙⲟϥ ⲙⲁⲣⲉϥⲥⲱⲧⲙ

## English draft

He said: A good man had a place of vines. He gave it to tenant farmers, so that they would work it and he would receive its fruit from them. He sent his servant, so that the tenant farmers would give him the fruit of the place of vines. They seized his servant; they beat him. It was a little more, and they would have killed him. The servant went; he told his master. His master said: Perhaps he did not know them. He sent another servant. The tenant farmers beat that other one too. Then the master sent his son. He said: Perhaps they will be ashamed before my son. Those tenant farmers, since they knew that he is the heir of the place of vines, seized him; they killed him. Whoever has ears, let him hear.

## Notes

- This logion opens ⲡⲉϫⲁϥ "He said," not "Jesus said"; the draft keeps "He said" and does not supply a fuller opening. That bare opening is the Coptic's own, as at Logion 60's opening scene.
- "A good man had a place of vines" renders ⲟⲩⲣⲱⲙⲉ ⲛⲭⲣⲏⲥⲧⲟⲥ ⲛⲉⲟⲩⲛⲧⲁϥ ⲛⲟⲩⲙⲁ ⲛⲉⲗⲟⲟⲗⲉ, with ⲣⲱⲙⲉ "man," the Greek loan ⲭⲣⲏⲥⲧⲟⲥ "good, useful, upright" (chrēstos — named here in full because the word looks like "Christ" in transliteration and is not that word in this sentence; it describes the man), ⲙⲁ "place," and ⲉⲗⲟⲟⲗⲉ "grapes, vines." Fork: ⲙⲁ ⲛⲉⲗⲟⲟⲗⲉ is literally "a place of grapes/vines"; the draft keeps "place of vines" in all three occurrences rather than switching to the smoother single word "vineyard," so the repetition the Coptic carries stays visible, and the vineyard road is named here only.
- "He gave it to tenant farmers, so that they would work it and he would receive its fruit from them" renders ⲁϥⲧⲁⲁϥ ⲛϩⲉⲛⲟⲩⲟⲉⲓⲉ ϩⲓⲛⲁ ⲉⲩⲛⲁⲣϩⲱⲃ ⲉⲣⲟϥ ⲛϥϫⲓ ⲙⲡⲉϥⲕⲁⲣⲡⲟⲥ ⲛⲧⲟⲟⲧⲟⲩ, with ϯ "give," ⲟⲩⲟⲉⲓⲉ "cultivators, tenant farmers" kept as "tenant farmers" throughout, ⲣϩⲱⲃ "work, do work," ϫⲓ "receive, take," and the Greek loan ⲕⲁⲣⲡⲟⲥ "fruit" — the same fruit-word as Logion 63, kept the same way. The give-so-they-work-so-he-receives chain is kept in Coptic order.
- "They seized his servant; they beat him. It was a little more, and they would have killed him" renders ⲁⲩⲉⲙⲁϩⲧⲉ ⲙⲡⲉϥϩⲙϩⲁⲗ ⲁⲩϩⲓⲟⲩⲉ ⲉⲣⲟϥ ⲛⲉⲕⲉⲕⲟⲩⲓ ⲡⲉ ⲛⲥⲉⲙⲟⲟⲩⲧϥ, with ⲁⲙⲁϩⲧⲉ "seize, hold fast," ϩⲙϩⲁⲗ "servant" (as in Logion 64), ϩⲓⲟⲩⲉ "beat, strike," and ⲙⲟⲟⲩⲧ (ⲙⲟⲩⲟⲩⲧ) "kill." Fork: ⲛⲉⲕⲉⲕⲟⲩⲓ ⲡⲉ is literally "it was another little" — an idiom for "a little more and …"; the draft's "It was a little more, and they would have killed him" keeps that shape plainly and names the literal road here.
- "Perhaps he did not know them" renders ⲙⲉϣⲁⲕ ⲙⲡⲉϥⲥⲟⲩⲱⲛⲟⲩ, with the Greek-family adverb ⲙⲉϣⲁⲕ "perhaps," the negated past ⲙⲡⲉϥ-, and ⲥⲟⲩⲱⲛ (ⲥⲟⲟⲩⲛ) "know, recognize" — the same knowing-word as Logia 56 and 61's family, kept as "know." Fork: the road "perhaps they did not know him" reverses the pronouns; the Coptic's suffixes here are ⲙⲡⲉ-ϥ-ⲥⲟⲩⲱⲛ-ⲟⲩ, "he did not know them," and the draft follows those suffixes as written, naming the other road here rather than choosing it silently. The same ⲙⲉϣⲁⲕ "perhaps" returns before the son is sent and is rendered identically.
- "Perhaps they will be ashamed before my son" renders ⲙⲉϣⲁⲕ ⲥⲉⲛⲁϣⲓⲡⲉ ϩⲏⲧϥ ⲙⲡⲁϣⲏⲣⲉ, with ϣⲓⲡⲉ "be ashamed, feel shame," ϩⲏⲧϥ "before him," and ϣⲏⲣⲉ "son." Fork: ϣⲓⲡⲉ ϩⲏⲧ- can carry "respect, stand in awe of" as well as "be ashamed before"; the draft keeps "be ashamed before" and names the respect road here.
- "Since they knew that he is the heir of the place of vines, seized him; they killed him" renders ⲁⲛⲟⲩⲟⲉⲓⲉ ⲉⲧⲙⲙⲁⲩ ⲉⲡⲉⲓⲥⲉⲥⲟⲟⲩⲛ ϫⲉⲛⲧⲟϥ ⲡⲉ ⲡⲉⲕⲗⲏⲣⲟⲛⲟⲙⲟⲥ ⲙⲡⲙⲁ ⲛⲉⲗⲟⲟⲗⲉ ⲁⲩϭⲟⲡϥ ⲁⲩⲙⲟⲟⲩⲧϥ, with the Greek loan ⲕⲗⲏⲣⲟⲛⲟⲙⲟⲥ "heir," ϭⲟⲡ (ϭⲱⲡⲉ) "seize" — a second seize-word beside ⲁⲙⲁϩⲧⲉ above, kept distinct in this note though the draft uses "seized" for both in plain English — and ⲙⲟⲟⲩⲧ "kill" again. No burial, no return, and no word from the master after the killing is written in the Coptic; the logion passes straight to the ears formula, and the draft does the same.
- "Whoever has ears, let him hear" renders ⲡⲉⲧⲉⲩⲙⲙⲁⲁϫⲉ ⲙⲙⲟϥ ⲙⲁⲣⲉϥⲥⲱⲧⲙ — rendered identically with Logion 63's closing formula, each occurrence on its own worksheet, never merged.
- G1: checked sentence by sentence against base sentences s0254–s0265; good-man / place-of-vines given to tenants / first servant seized and beaten / perhaps-he-did-not-know-them / second servant beaten / son sent with perhaps-ashamed / heir known, seized, killed / ears formula order kept; no parallel citations added (PENDING ROBERT).

# Logion 66

- **Witness status:** Coptic-only
- **Parallels:** PENDING ROBERT
- **State:** DRAFTED (G1 self-audit done — see notes)

## Greek

No Greek witness — Coptic alone.

## Coptic (Coptic Scriptorium `thomas.gospel` v6.2.0, CC BY 4.0; base sentences s0266–s0267, normalized text as in the base file)

ⲡⲉϫⲉⲓⲏⲥⲟⲩⲥϫⲉⲙⲁⲧⲥⲁⲃⲟⲓⲉⲡⲱⲛⲉⲡⲁⲓⲛⲧⲁⲩⲥⲧⲟϥⲉⲃⲟⲗⲛϭⲓⲛⲉⲧⲕⲱⲧ ⲛⲧⲟϥⲡⲉⲡⲱⲛⲉⲛⲕⲱϩ

## English draft

Jesus said: Show me the stone that the builders threw away; it is the corner stone.

## Notes

- ⲙⲁ ⲧⲥⲁⲃⲟ ⲓ "make me know" — the draft's "Show me" is the plain-English carrying of that causative; fork: "Teach me" is the other honest road, named here, not chosen.
- "the stone that the builders threw away" renders ⲡⲱⲛⲉ ⲡⲁⲓ ⲛⲧⲁⲩⲥⲧⲟϥ ⲉⲃⲟⲗ ⲛϭⲓ ⲛⲉⲧⲕⲱⲧ, with the demonstrative ⲡⲁⲓ "this" after the noun, the relative past ⲛⲧⲁⲩ-, ⲥⲧⲟ ϥ ⲉⲃⲟⲗ "reject, cast away" (kept as "threw away" in the plain register), and ⲛⲉⲧⲕⲱⲧ "the ones who build" = "the builders."
- "it is the corner stone" renders ⲛⲧⲟϥ ⲡⲉ ⲡⲱⲛⲉ ⲛⲕⲱϩ — ⲱⲛⲉ "stone," ⲕⲱϩ "corner." Fork: "head of the corner" / "keystone" is the other road; the draft keeps the literal "corner stone" and names it.
- G1: checked sentence by sentence against base sentences s0266–s0267; show-me clause, builders clause, corner-stone identification kept in Coptic order; no Greek printed or invented; no parallel citations added (PENDING ROBERT).

> DRAFT — working translation from the Coptic (and Greek where it survives), prepared with AI assistance; not reviewed by a Coptic scholar; not finished. Corrections invited and credited.

# Logion 67

- **Witness status:** Coptic-only
- **Parallels:** PENDING ROBERT
- **State:** DRAFTED (G1 self-audit done — see notes)

## Greek

No Greek witness — Coptic alone.

## Coptic (Coptic Scriptorium `thomas.gospel` v6.2.0, CC BY 4.0; base sentence s0268, normalized text as in the base file)

ⲡⲉϫⲉⲓⲏⲥⲟⲩⲥϫⲉⲡⲉⲧⲥⲟⲟⲩⲛⲙⲡⲧⲏⲣϥⲉϥⲣϭⲣⲱϩⲟⲩⲁⲁϥϥⲣϭⲣⲱϩⲙⲡⲙⲁⲧⲏⲣϥ

## English draft

Jesus said: Whoever knows everything but falls short in one thing, falls short in everything.

## Notes

- "Whoever knows everything" renders ⲡⲉⲧⲥⲟⲟⲩⲛ ⲙⲡⲧⲏⲣϥ — the knowing-word ⲥⲟⲟⲩⲛ, consistent with Logia 3, 56, 61, and 65, kept as "know" everywhere; ⲡⲧⲏⲣϥ "the whole, everything."
- "falls short" renders ⲣϭⲣⲱϩ in both occurrences, kept identical rather than varied away. Fork: ⲣϭⲣⲱϩ also carries "be poor, be lacking" — the poverty road is named here, not taken, so the draft stays checkable against the Coptic.
- The first half is a circumstantial: ⲉϥⲣϭⲣⲱϩ ⲟⲩⲁⲁϥ "being short of one (thing)" — "but falls short in one thing" carries that condition plainly. The second half ⲣϭⲣⲱϩ ⲙⲡⲙⲁ ⲧⲏⲣϥ is literally "falls short in every place" = "in everything"; the draft keeps "in everything" and names the literal road here.
- Reader-lens fence: Robert's stated resonance on this logion (knowing without inner substance as poverty) is recorded in the audit as reader-lens only; it did not choose or bend any word above — the poverty fork is footnoted, not adopted.
- G1: checked sentence by sentence against base sentence s0268; one balanced saying, two halves in Coptic order; no Greek printed or invented; no parallel citations added (PENDING ROBERT).

# Logion 68

- **Witness status:** Coptic-only
- **Parallels:** PENDING ROBERT
- **State:** DRAFTED (G1 self-audit done — see notes)

## Greek

No Greek witness — Coptic alone.

## Coptic (Coptic Scriptorium `thomas.gospel` v6.2.0, CC BY 4.0; base sentences s0269–s0270, normalized text as in the base file)

ⲡⲉϫⲉⲓⲏⲥⲟⲩⲥϫⲉⲛⲧⲱⲧⲛϩⲉⲙⲙⲁⲕⲁⲣⲓⲟⲥϩⲟⲧⲁⲛⲉⲩϣⲁⲛⲙⲉⲥⲧⲉⲧⲏⲩⲧⲛⲛⲥⲉⲣⲇⲓⲱⲕⲉⲙⲙⲱⲧⲛ ⲁⲩⲱⲥⲉⲛⲁϩⲉⲁⲛⲉⲧⲟⲡⲟⲥϩⲙⲡⲙⲁⲉⲛⲧⲁⲩⲇⲓⲱⲕⲉⲙⲙⲱⲧⲛϩⲣⲁⲓⲛϩⲏⲧϥ

## English draft

Jesus said: You are blessed when they hate you and persecute you; and they will find no place in the place where they persecuted you.

## Notes

- "You are blessed" renders ⲛⲧⲱⲧⲛ ϩⲉⲙ ⲙⲁⲕⲁⲣⲓⲟⲥ — the ⲙⲁⲕⲁⲣⲓⲟⲥ "blessed" kept identical with Logia 49, 54, and 58; ⲛⲧⲱⲧⲛ "you (plural)."
- "hate" renders ⲙⲉⲥⲧⲉ, kept as "hate" as at Logion 55 — hard, not softened.
- "persecute" renders ⲇⲓⲱⲕⲉ, the Greek loan διώκειν; first occurrence on the worksheets, so the loan is named here. The when-clause keeps Coptic order: hate first, persecute second.
- "they will find no place in the place" renders ⲥⲉ ⲛⲁ ϩⲉ ⲁⲛ ⲉ ⲧⲟⲡⲟⲥ ϩⲙ ⲡⲙⲁ — the Greek loan ⲧⲟⲡⲟⲥ and the native ⲙⲁ both rendered "place," named here rather than varied; the repetition the Coptic carries stays visible. ϩⲣⲁⲓ ⲛϩⲏⲧϥ "in it" is carried by the "there" of the plain "where they persecuted you."
- G1: checked sentence by sentence against base sentences s0269–s0270; blessed-when clause then no-place clause in Coptic order; no Greek printed or invented; no parallel citations added (PENDING ROBERT).

# Logion 69

- **Witness status:** Coptic-only
- **Parallels:** PENDING ROBERT
- **State:** DRAFTED (G1 self-audit done — see notes)

## Greek

No Greek witness — Coptic alone.

## Coptic (Coptic Scriptorium `thomas.gospel` v6.2.0, CC BY 4.0; base sentences s0271–s0273, normalized text as in the base file)

ⲡⲉϫⲉⲓⲏⲥⲟⲩⲥϩⲉⲙⲙⲁⲕⲁⲣⲓⲟⲥⲛⲉⲛⲁⲓⲛⲧⲁⲩⲇⲓⲱⲕⲉⲙⲙⲟⲟⲩϩⲣⲁⲓϩⲙⲡⲉⲩϩⲏⲧ ⲛⲉⲧⲙⲙⲁⲩⲛⲉⲛⲧⲁϥⲥⲟⲩⲱⲛⲡⲉⲓⲱⲧϩⲛⲟⲩⲙⲉ ϩⲉⲙⲙⲁⲕⲁⲣⲓⲟⲥⲛⲉⲧϩⲕⲁⲉⲓⲧϩⲓⲛⲁⲉⲩⲛⲁⲧⲥⲓⲟⲛⲧϩⲏⲙⲡⲉⲧⲟⲩⲱϣ

## English draft

Jesus said: Blessed are those who were persecuted in their hearts — they are the ones who have come to know the Father in truth. Blessed are the hungry, so that the stomach of the one who desires will be filled.

## Notes

- "those who were persecuted in their hearts" renders ⲛⲁⲓ ⲛⲧⲁⲩⲇⲓⲱⲕⲉ ⲙⲙⲟⲟⲩ ϩⲣⲁⲓ ϩⲙ ⲡⲉⲩϩⲏⲧ — the persecute-loan ⲇⲓⲱⲕⲉ as at Logion 68, with the reflexive ⲙⲙⲟⲟⲩ and "in their heart" kept plain; fork: "persecuted within themselves" is the other honest road, named here.
- "they are the ones who have come to know the Father in truth" renders ⲛⲉⲧⲙⲙⲁⲩ ⲛⲉⲛⲧⲁϥⲥⲟⲩⲱⲛ ⲡⲉⲓⲱⲧ ϩⲛ ⲟⲩⲙⲉ — the identification sentence ⲛⲉⲧⲙⲙⲁⲩ "these are they," the knowing-word ⲥⲟⲟⲩⲛ kept as "know" with the past ⲛⲧⲁ- "have come to know," and ⲟⲩⲙⲉ "truth" kept plain. This is the same knowing-word as Logia 3, 56, 61, 65, and 67 — rendered identically.
- "Blessed are the hungry" renders ϩⲉⲙ ⲙⲁⲕⲁⲣⲓⲟⲥ ⲛⲉⲧϩⲕⲁⲉⲓⲧ — ⲙⲁⲕⲁⲣⲓⲟⲥ "blessed" kept identical with Logia 49, 54, 58, and 68; ⲛⲉⲧϩⲕⲁⲉⲓⲧ "the ones who hunger."
- "so that the stomach of the one who desires will be filled" renders ϩⲓⲛⲁ ⲉⲩⲛⲁⲧⲥⲓⲟ ⲛⲧϩⲏ ⲙⲡⲉⲧⲟⲩⲱϣ — the purpose clause ϩⲓⲛⲁ kept as "so that," ⲧⲥⲓⲟ "be filled, satisfied," ⲛⲉϩ "belly, stomach" kept literal ("stomach") rather than smoothed into the hungry — named here as the fork not taken — and ⲡⲉⲧⲟⲩⲱϣ "the one who wants, desires."
- This logion's blessed pair is kept entirely on its own worksheet; Logion 68's blessed saying is not imported or merged in.
- G1: checked sentence by sentence against base sentences s0271–s0273; three sentences in Coptic order; no Greek printed or invented; no parallel citations added (PENDING ROBERT).

# Logion 70

- **Witness status:** Coptic-only
- **Parallels:** PENDING ROBERT
- **State:** DRAFTED (G1 self-audit done — see notes)

## Greek

No Greek witness — Coptic alone.

## Coptic (Coptic Scriptorium `thomas.gospel` v6.2.0, CC BY 4.0; base sentences s0274–s0275, normalized text as in the base file)

ⲡⲉϫⲉⲓⲏⲥⲟⲩⲥϩⲟⲧⲁⲛⲉⲧⲉⲧⲛϣⲁⲛϫⲡⲉⲡⲏϩⲛⲧⲏⲩⲧⲛⲡⲁⲓⲉⲧⲉⲩⲛⲧⲏⲧⲛϥϥⲛⲁⲧⲟⲩϫⲉⲧⲏⲩⲧⲛ ⲉϣⲱⲡⲉⲙⲛⲧⲏⲧⲛⲡⲏϩⲛⲧⲏⲩⲧⲛⲡⲁⲓⲉⲧⲉⲙⲛⲧⲏⲧⲛϥϩⲛⲧⲏⲛⲉϥⲛⲁⲙⲉⲩⲧⲧⲏⲛⲉ

## English draft

Jesus said: When you bring forth what is within you, what you have will save you. If you do not have what is within you, what you do not have will kill you.

## Notes

- "bring forth" renders ϫⲡⲉ "produce, bring forth" — fork: "give birth to" is the other honest road, named here; the draft keeps the broader plain verb.
- "what is within you" renders ⲡⲏ ϩⲛ ⲧⲏⲩⲧⲛ in both halves, rendered identically both times. The Coptic does not say what "that within you" is, and the draft does not supply it — reader-lens readings stay fenced out of the rendering.
- "what you have will save you" renders ⲡⲁⲓ ⲉⲧⲉ ⲩⲛⲧⲏⲧⲛ ϥ ϥⲛⲁⲧⲟⲩϫⲉ ⲧⲏⲩⲧⲛ — ⲩⲛⲧⲏⲧⲛ "have, possess" and ⲧⲟⲩϫⲉ "save," first occurrence on the worksheets, so the save-word is named here.
- "what you do not have will kill you" renders ⲡⲁⲓ ⲉⲧⲉ ⲙⲛⲧⲏⲧⲛ ϥ ϩⲛ ⲧⲏⲛⲉ ϥⲛⲁⲙⲉⲩⲧ ⲧⲏⲛⲉ — the negative-have construction ⲙⲛⲧⲏⲧⲛ "you do not have (it)" kept plain, and ⲙⲟⲩⲟⲩⲧ "kill" kept as "kill," consistent with Logia 56, 60, and 65. The balanced when/if halves are kept in Coptic order; neither is smoothed into the other.
- G1: checked sentence by sentence against base sentences s0274–s0275; bring-forth/save half then not-have/kill half in Coptic order; no Greek printed or invented; no parallel citations added (PENDING ROBERT).

# Logion 71

- **Witness status:** Coptic-only
- **Parallels:** PENDING ROBERT
- **State:** DRAFTED (G1 self-audit done — see notes)

## Greek

No Greek witness — Coptic alone.

## Coptic (Coptic Scriptorium `thomas.gospel` v6.2.0, CC BY 4.0; base sentences s0276–s0277, normalized text as in the base file)

ⲡⲉϫⲉ ⲓⲏⲥⲟⲩⲥ ϫⲉ ϯⲛⲁϣⲟⲣϣⲣ ⲙⲡⲉⲓⲏⲓ ⲁⲩⲱ ⲙⲛⲗⲁⲁⲩ ⲛⲁϣⲕⲟⲧϥ [........]

## English draft

Jesus said: I will destroy this house, and no one will be able to build it [again — the ending is lost].

## Notes

- "destroy" renders ϣⲟⲣϣⲣ "destroy, tear down, dissolve" — fork: "tear down" is the other honest road; the draft keeps the plainest verb.

- The saying's ending is genuinely lost: eight dots of manuscript damage follow ⲙⲛ ⲗⲁⲁⲩ ⲛⲁ ϣ ⲕⲟⲧ ϥ ("and no one will build it"). The draft renders the recoverable clause and brackets the loss; nothing is invented to fill it.
- "this house" renders ⲡⲉⲓ ⲏⲓ with the near demonstrative; "no one" renders the negative existential ⲙⲛ ⲗⲁⲁⲩ, kept plain as in Logia 40 and 65.
- G1: checked sentence by sentence against base sentences s0276–s0277; future-threat clause then no-one-can-build clause in Coptic order; no Greek printed or invented; no parallel citations added (PENDING ROBERT).

# Logion 72

- **Witness status:** Coptic-only
- **Parallels:** PENDING ROBERT
- **State:** DRAFTED (G1 self-audit done — see notes)

## Greek

No Greek witness — Coptic alone.

## Coptic (Coptic Scriptorium `thomas.gospel` v6.2.0, CC BY 4.0; base sentences s0278–s0280, normalized text as in the base file)

ⲡⲉϫⲉ ⲟⲩⲣⲱⲙⲉ ⲛⲁϥ ϫⲉ ϫⲟⲟⲥ ⲛⲛⲁⲥⲛⲏⲩ ϩⲓⲛⲁ ⲉⲩⲛⲁⲡⲱϣⲉ ⲛⲛϩⲛⲁⲁⲩ ⲙⲡⲁⲉⲓⲱⲧ ⲛⲙⲙⲁⲓ. ⲡⲉϫⲁϥ ⲛⲁϥ ϫⲉ ⲱ ⲡⲣⲱⲙⲉ ⲛⲓⲙ ⲡⲉ ⲛⲧⲁϥⲁⲁⲧⲛ ⲣⲉϥⲡⲱϣⲉ. ⲁϥⲕⲟⲧϥ ⲉⲛⲉϥⲙⲁⲑⲏⲧⲏⲥ ⲡⲉϫⲁϥ ⲛⲁⲩ ϫⲉ ⲙⲏ ⲉⲓϣⲟⲟⲡ ⲛⲣⲉϥⲡⲱϣⲉ.

## English draft

A man said to him: Tell my brothers to divide my father's things with me. He said to him: O man, who made me a divider? He turned to his disciples; he said to them: Am I a divider?

## Notes

- "divide" renders ⲡⲱϣⲉ and "divider" the ⲣⲉϥ- agent noun ⲣⲉϥⲡⲱϣⲉ "one who divides" — both forks ("split" / "one who splits") named here; the draft keeps the plainest pair and uses it uniformly all three times.
- "my father's things" renders ⲛϩⲛⲁⲁⲩ ⲙⲡⲁⲉⲓⲱⲧ — ϩⲛⲁⲁⲩ "things, goods, vessels"; fork: "property" is the other honest road, named here. The draft keeps "things," the plainer word.
- "Who made me a divider?" renders ⲛⲓⲙ ⲡⲉ ⲛⲧⲁϥⲁⲁⲧⲛ ⲣⲉϥⲡⲱϣⲉ — the manuscript has a damaged letter in the relative verb (ϩ̣ in the base), flagged here; the reading is secure and the flag is the honesty.
- "He turned to his disciples" renders ⲁϥⲕⲟⲧϥ ⲉⲛⲉϥⲙⲁⲑⲏⲧⲏⲥ — ⲕⲟⲧϥ "turn, turn back"; the pivot to the disciples is a physical turn in the Coptic, kept literal.
- "Am I a divider?" renders the ⲙⲏ interrogative ⲙⲏ ⲉⲓϣⲟⲟⲡ ⲛⲣⲉϥⲡⲱϣⲉ — a question, not a denial; the draft does not answer it for him.
- The three speeches stay in Coptic order: the man's request, the answer to the man, the turn to the disciples. Nothing is collapsed or re-sequenced.
- G1: checked sentence by sentence against base sentences s0278–s0280; no Greek printed or invented; no parallel citations added (PENDING ROBERT).

# Logion 73

- **Witness status:** Coptic-only
- **Parallels:** PENDING ROBERT
- **State:** DRAFTED (G1 self-audit done — see notes)

## Greek

No Greek witness — Coptic alone.

## Coptic (Coptic Scriptorium `thomas.gospel` v6.2.0, CC BY 4.0; base sentences s0281–s0283, normalized text as in the base file)

ⲡⲉϫⲉ ⲓⲏⲥⲟⲩⲥ ϫⲉ ⲡⲱϩⲥ ⲙⲉⲛ ⲛⲁϣⲱϥ ⲛⲉⲣⲅⲁⲧⲏⲥ ⲇⲉ ⲥⲟⲃⲕ. ⲥⲟⲡⲥ ⲇⲉ ⲙⲡϫⲟⲉⲓⲥ ϩⲓⲛⲁ ⲉϥⲛⲁⲛⲉϫ ⲉⲣⲅⲁⲧⲏⲥ ⲉⲃⲟⲗ ⲉⲡⲱϩⲥ.

## English draft

Jesus said: The harvest is great, but the workers are few. Pray to the Lord that he send workers out into the harvest.

## Notes

- "harvest" renders ⲱϩⲥ, the standard Coptic word for harvest — used uniformly all three times, not varied away.
- "workers" renders the Greek loan ἐργάτης (ⲉⲣⲅⲁⲧⲏⲥ) — fork: "laborers" is the other honest road; the draft keeps the plain word a humble reader knows. The ⲙⲉⲛ … ⲇⲉ "indeed … but" balance is kept in Coptic order.
- "Pray" renders ⲥⲟⲡⲥ "beg, beseech, pray" — fork: "beg" is the other honest road, named here; the draft keeps "pray."
- "the Lord" renders ⲡϫⲟⲉⲓⲥ (master, lord); the draft does not resolve which lord — the Coptic does not.
- "send out" renders ⲛⲟⲩϫⲉ ⲉⲃⲟⲗ "cast/throw out, send forth" — fork: "thrust out" is the other honest road; the draft keeps the plain verb.
- G1: checked sentence by sentence against base sentences s0281–s0283; no Greek printed or invented; no parallel citations added (PENDING ROBERT).
